# Design decisions (pedagogy and musical content)

Decisions taken by the project owner; code must follow them.

| id | decision | effect in code |
|---|---|---|
| D0 | Corrected kern reading (version of record) is canonical for Figs. 22, 85b, 86a, 87b. | `CANONICAL_READING = "kern_vor"` |
| D1 | Fux's own C cantus firmi may be offered with the player's counterpoint, without an original solution. | curriculum steps 11-12 (`fux_cf_c_01`, after "Perge ergo ad A. & C.", p. 55) |
| D2 | Superseded by D39: voice crossing is not checked. | `fs.no-voice-crossing` severity `warning` |
| D3 | ~~Keep the descending-minor-sixth ban for now.~~ Superseded by D11. | — |
| D4 | ~~The unattested preferences stay as warnings.~~ Superseded by D11. | — |
| D5 | **Fux mode follows the book: rules enter the game in Fux's order of presentation.** | `src/counterpoint/curriculum/fux-first-species.ts` |
| D6 | No Ionian-specific gameplay rules; Fux mode is faithful to Fux. | `fs.ionian-no-accidentals` removed |
| D7 | Opening as Fux states it: perfect consonance; with the counterpoint below, octave or unison only. Cadence: M6 (cantus below) / m3 (cantus above) to octave or unison. | `fs.opening-perfect`, `fs.cadence` voicing-aware |
| D8 | Generator constraints of M1-REPORT §4 accepted. | generator not yet implemented (M4) |
| D9 | Level 1 follows the book: cantus below and above interleaved from Fig. 6 on. | curriculum steps 1-12 |
| D10 | A rule is unchecked before Fux introduces or notes it; from that step on it is checked. | `rulesForStep()` |
| D11 | Rules whose introduction cannot be found in Fux's first-species text (pp. 45, 47-55) are dropped for now: descending m6, sevenths, leaps beyond the octave, other augmented/diminished leaps, preference for imperfect consonances, avoiding successive leaps. | removed from fux-strict |
| D12 | The player is Josephus and writes every counterpoint; Josephus's faulty first versions are not staged. | — |

Note: the page supplied as "p. 46" is p. 46 of Mann's English translation (second species, Figs. 36-38), not the 1725 p. 46; per the owner's instruction, Mann's text is not used.
| D13 | Default ("modern") clefs are the per-exercise modern clefs given in the dataset (e.g. treble over treble for Fig. 5), not a fixed treble over bass. | `exerciseView().clefs.modern` |
| D14 | Two switchable sounds: sampled piano and an 8-bit square-wave synthesizer (organ dropped). | `src/audio/engine.ts`, Piano / 8-bit buttons |
| D15 | 8-bit sound only for now; the piano option is hidden (code kept). | `PIANO_ENABLED = false` in `src/ui/App.tsx` |
| D16 | Evaluation shows compound intervals in simple form (m10 → m3, P12 → P5, P15 → P8). | `simpleName()` in overlay and feedback |
| D17 | Hints name only that an accidental is needed at the cadence, not the note. | `Hints.tsx` |
| D18 | One star per exercise, earned by clearing it (all rules), shown at the top left of the score. | `wtg.stars` |
| D19 | Exercise navigation in book order (12 steps) beside the title. | `App.tsx` |
| D20 | Original-clefs mode removed from the UI (data kept). | — |
| D21 | "More imperfect than perfect consonances, beginning and end excepted" is Fux's (1725 p. 46, read via Mann p. 28): restored as a recommendation from Fig. 5, checked as "perfect must not outnumber imperfect" because Fux's own Fig. 11 ties 4–4. Mann's footnote rules (leaps > 5th, successive leaps, repeated notes) are Mann's, not Fux's, and stay out. | `fs.prefer-imperfect-consonances` |
| D22 | Synth models: subtractive, plucked string (Karplus–Strong), FM, additive; 12 presets. | `src/audio/engine.ts` |
| D23 | Cantus firmus and counterpoint have independent synth settings; the rack edits Both / Counterpoint / Cantus firmus. | `SynthRack.tsx` |
| D24 | "If Fux calls it a rule, it's a rule": `fs.prefer-imperfect-consonances` is an error. | severity `error` |
| D25 | ~~A star requires a clean result (no rule and no recommendation broken).~~ Superseded by D143. | `App.tsx` |
| D26 | Synth: 9 models (analog/subtractive, plucked string, FM, additive, formant voices, bowed string, wavetable, ring modulator, wavefolder), 23 presets shown per model; per-voice reverb (room, hall, cathedral, plate, spring) and delay (digital, analog, tape, slapback, ping-pong). | `src/audio/` |
| D27 | "Both voices" edits move both voices relatively and never overwrite choices on which they differ. | `applyEdit()` |
| D28 | Drum track toggle (kick, snare, hi-hats, crash, tom fill) during playback. Temperament toggle: equal, Pythagorean, ¼-comma meantone, Werckmeister III (spelled-pitch aware, anchored on D). | `drums.ts`, `temperament.ts` |
| D29 | Fux comparison: each difference is weighed by Fux's stated criteria (p. 45 motion, p. 46 imperfect consonances, p. 53 ease of singing), crediting whichever side they favour. | `compare-fux.ts` |
| D30 | Supersedes D13. Display clefs are G or F only, per staff: G when the voice averages at or above middle C, F otherwise (the counterpoint's register is estimated as the cantus an octave away, never from Fux's solution). Owner: "a few exercises would better have a F clef". Dataset clefs are untouched. | `displayClefs()` in `exercise-view.ts` |
| D31 | Second species carries over from first species only what Fux says carries ("motion and progression", 1725 p. 56) and what his examples obey: rules of motion, opening and final, melodic tritone and major sixth, converging leap, crossing (warning), contrary motion (warning). Not carried: unison only at the ends ("in this species", p. 54), more imperfect than perfect (p. 46), unison leap (contradicted by Figs. 36, 39, 41, 42). | `rules/second-species.ts` |
| D32 | Supersedes D12. The player does whatever Aloysius tasks the pupil with: exercises Josephus writes are tasks; exercises Aloysius writes as models (e.g. Figs. 82-83, 101, 104-105, 121-123) are to be studied, not written. In two-voice first and second species every exercise is Josephus's. | `docs/fux/catalogue.md` ("Written by") |
| D33 | Level selector: voices → species → exercise; unimplemented courses shown disabled. A second-species exercise starts with Fux's half rest in place; R (or the Rest button) restores it after a note is written there. | `curriculum/index.ts`, `App.tsx` |
| D34 | Drums open an options box: on/off, loop length ×2 / ÷2 (¼ to 4 bars), level, and patterns in four families (kit, percussion, tribal, orchestral; timpani tuned to the final and its fifth). | `audio/drums.ts`, `DrumBox.tsx` |
| D35 | A precept stays in force unless Fux's own examples break it (owner). Second species: unison only at the ends checked on downbeats (Fux has upbeat unisons only); more imperfect than perfect becomes a recommendation (Fig. 36 breaks it); unison leap dropped (Figs. 36, 39, 41, 42). | `rules/second-species.ts` |
| D36 | Supersedes D27. Synth voice menu: Both voices (default; one shared configuration), Cantus firmus, Contrapunctus. Returning to Both voices from a single voice asks for confirmation and gives both the Contrapunctus settings. Choosing a model loads its first preset, which is always clean (no reverb, no delay). Default sound: modelled piano, "Glenn Gould" preset. | `SynthRack.tsx`, `synth-settings.ts` |
| D37 | Fux's solution (score overlay in diamonds, comparison, Fux and Trio playback) opens only once the exercise is cleared. No feedback cues. | `App.tsx` |
| D38 | Exercises are named (study.en.json), not numbered by figure; the figure stays in the source line. Hints split into Rules (with "Specific to this exercise") and Lectio (mode, Fux's text with our translation, paraphrased dialogue, Mann's footnotes quoted and attributed). | `Hints.tsx`, `Study.tsx`, `content/study.en.json` |
| D39 | "Fux is always the last word": no rule or recommendation may fire on Fux's own solution at its step. The second-species imperfect-consonance recommendation is dropped (Fig. 36). The crossing warning (D2) is removed too: it fired on Figs. 14, 37, 39. | `rules/second-species.ts` |
| D40 | Supersedes D36's voice menu. Sound drawer = mixer: strips for Cantus firmus, Contrapunctus, Fux, Drums and Master (volume, balance, mute, solo; Fux octave -3..+3 for listening only; tuning on the master). Clicking a strip's instrument opens its synth (or the drum options) below. Chain buttons link neighbouring voices into one shared sound; linking voices that differ asks first and gives them the Contrapunctus sound. Defaults: cantus and contrapunctus linked on the Glenn Gould piano; Fux unlinked on "Fux's harpsichord". | `audio/sound.ts`, `SoundDesk.tsx` |
| D41 | Interface: controls grouped Write · Evaluate · Listen; Rules and Lectio as tabs below; "?" help card replaces the help lines; no inactive-score state; "Next exercise" after clearing; species progress in the selector; dark theme (system, or forced from the header). | `App.tsx`, `styles.css` |
| D42 | Default sound: recorded grand piano (Salamander, CC BY 3.0, two velocity layers); Fux's channel: recorded pipe organ (VSCO 2 CE via tonejs-instruments). Sackbut and cello offered; synthesis models remain. Samples live in public/samples and are published beside the page. | `audio/voice.ts`, `sample-manifest.ts` |
| D43 | Aloysius opens each exercise with a line from Fux (1725 Latin, our translation, page cited). View toggles: note names, unjudged intervals. Loop on by default. The Fux comparison credits neither oblique motion nor singability obtained by repeating a note, and credits variety (1725 p. 74). | `study.en.json`, `App.tsx`, `compare-fux.ts` |
| D44 | Basso continuo integrated. A Continuo button sits in the Listen group after Sound (the drum switch lives inside the Sound drawer, so "after Drums" became "after Sound") and opens a panel beside the sound options: on/off, level, score display, instruments (stile antico, cembalo, Hofkapelle), finals (organist / strict), passing fills, inégal (a stylistic liberty, never with stile antico). Demo mode (`CONTINUO_DEMO_MODE`): it plays and shows whatever is written, solved or not; empty slots are rests. Player and Fux playback are realized; the trio is pure doubling (no harmony added, figures = the sung intervals over the bass). Score display: none, figured bass (default), realization, realization + figured bass; cue-sized staves labelled "B.c." in a warm grey of their own, below the two staves, which never move. The continuo bass sits an octave below the lowest sung voice. Generated for pleasure: no part in grading. | `src/game/continuo-*.ts`, `ContinuoBox.tsx`, `ScoreView.tsx`, `src/continuo/` |
| D45 | Inversion and retrograde of the player's line (Inv, Retr, both) are toggles at the foot of the Contrapunctus strip, for listening only, like Fux's octave: playback and the continuo follow the heard line; the score and the evaluation keep the written one. Inversion is diatonic, about the line's first note, accidentals dropped; retrograde keeps the rhythm and the rests in place. | `game/transform.ts`, `SoundDesk.tsx` |
| D46 | Amends D40/D44. No Sound or Continuo button: "Mixer & synth" is always on the page and folds to one line, its button at the far left. The continuo is a mixer strip between Drums and Master (level, balance, mute, solo, on/off, score display); its options open in the editor below like any strip's. Clicking a strip anywhere but on its controls selects it. The editor below the desk has one fixed size for every strip. Synth navigation: family ‹ › then preset ‹ ›, side by side; the preset list column is gone. | `SoundDesk.tsx`, `SynthRack.tsx`, `sound.ts` |
| D47 | Supersedes D45. Versions of the player's line: O (as written), I (inversion), R (retrograde), RI, C (canon at the unison, displaced by x slots and wrapping round to the first bars; x = 0 duplicates). Toggles on the Contrapunctus strip; each active version gets its own staff (read-only, own ink) and its own mixer strip (sharing the Contrapunctus sound); O off puts the first version on the player's staff and locks editing. Only O is written; the others are derived from it. Inversion is Bach's tonal mirror (owner): final ↔ fifth, third fixed, natural notes stay natural, an inflected note mirrors its semitone (C♯ → B♭ in D), placed in the original's register. Evaluate judges each active version against the cantus as well. In two-voice exercises, with Fux's solution shown, a three-voice reading of cantus + player + Fux lists where the texture breaks (clashes between the two counterpoints, parallel perfects between them) and where it works (complete triads, contrary motion), without a grade. The Fux comparison no longer lists differences no stated criterion separates (taste). | `game/versions.ts`, `game/trio-eval.ts`, `TrioReading.tsx`, `SoundDesk.tsx`, `ScoreView.tsx` |
| D48 | F-mode exercises carry a one-flat key signature and a plain B is B♭ (every B in Fux's F-mode lines, Figs. 14, 38, 39, is flat; the cantus has none). The ♮ button gives B♮; pressing it again returns to the key. B♮ is not itself flagged. | `exercise-view.ts` (`signature`), `session.ts`, `ScoreView.tsx` |
| D49 | Saved pieces. A floppy-disk button in the header saves a snapshot: exercise, the written line, its versions, the playback (Fux's line or the trio when Fux is shown or playing), the whole mixer and synths, drums and kit, continuo and its settings, tuning, tempo and volume. A ♫ button opens the saved pieces, lined up as tracks: play/stop with a contour "waveform" and progress, rename (click the title), Open (loads it all back into the game), delete. A saved piece plays with its own settings and plays once; the live setup returns when it stops. Kept per browser (localStorage `wtg.saved`). | `game/saved.ts`, `SavedPieces.tsx`, `ui/playback.ts` |
| D50 | Supersedes D48 (owner: "follow Fux's way"). No key signature in the F mode: B♮ is the mode's note, and Fux's B♭s are accidentals used against *mi contra fa*, "not essential to the diatonic genus" (1725 pp. 68-69). The F-mode exercises say so in plain words under "Specific to this exercise". The signature mechanism stays in the code, unused. | `exercise-view.ts`, `study.en.json` |
| D51 | DEMO button in the header opens a demo area: five entries, each loading Fux's own solution already judged (Fux shown; his playback, the comparison and the three-voice reading open; no star): first species D (cantus below), first species F (cantus above), second species D (below), second species F (above), and second species E with the continuo on (cembalo, realization + figured bass). Each lists, in plain words, what to try. | `game/demo.ts`, `App.tsx` |
| D52 | Owner requests: browsing dwell 0.5 s; perfect intervals written without "P" (1, 4, 5, 8; M/m/A/d kept); type: EB Garamond for prose, Inter for controls, labels, note names and numbers in the score, Garamond italic for figures and voice labels; Fux's default sound is "Flute organ" (the organ samples darkened to a stopped-flute colour, softer speech, a little hall; a stored state still on the old pipe organ moves to it); every box (demo, evaluation, each version's evaluation, Fux's solution, three-voice reading) folds to one line like Mixer & synth, remembered per box. | `App.tsx`, `interval.ts`, `styles.css`, `synth-settings.ts`, `Fold.tsx` |
| D53 | Humanised playback, switched in the demo area (off by default; applies to every "play all", saved pieces included): metric accent, phrase arch, high-loud, detached repeated notes, a breath before leaps of a fourth or more, final ritardando (the continuo follows it), small deterministic irregularities. A reduced selection of the KTH rule system (Friberg, Bresin & Sundberg 2006). The demo area also lists every exercise with Fux's solution, each playable at once. | `audio/humanise.ts`, `engine.ts`, `App.tsx` |
| D54 | Recorded instruments in two families at the top of the synth's family list: Classic (recorded): grand piano, flute organ, pipe organ, sackbut, cello, violin, flute, bassoon, French horn, trumpet, harp, double bass, harmonium, classical guitar; Modern (recorded): electric guitar, electric bass, saxophone, xylophone. From tonejs-instruments (VSCO 2 CE, Freesound, Karoryfer; CC BY 3.0), one sample per minor third or closer, levels matched. `tools/sample-manifest.mjs` regenerates the manifest. | `public/samples`, `synth-settings.ts`, `voice.ts` |
| D55 | Mann's layer in the Fux comparison: for each difference, "Mann would have scored yours / Fux's higher:" by Alfred Mann's melodic advice in his footnotes (1965, p. 27 n. 1, p. 29 n. 3): no leap larger than a fifth (octave and rising minor sixth excepted), no two leaps the same way, leaps compensated, no tone thrice. Attributed to Mann, never to Fux; a difference is listed if either Fux's criteria or Mann's separate the two choices. | `compare-fux.ts`, `FuxComparison.tsx` |
| D56 | The continuo plays as a partimento player (owner: "option 1"): A6 adds prepared suspensions (4-3, 7-6, 9-8, chains allowed when the bass falls by step), 5-6 over a bass about to rise by step, the rule of the octave's 6/5 on the fourth degree rising and the seventh rising to the final (when its fifth can resolve down), and a cadential trill on the leading tone (from the upper note, on the beat). No device may make a second, seventh or unison with a sung upper voice. Right-hand window F3–C5 (not above c²: Werckmeister; Niedt f²; Heinichen c³ by exception). Stile antico organ redrawn as a continuo chamber organ: Gedackt 8' alone in the right hand, softer, slightly detached; bass Gedackt 8' + a quiet 4'; gentler speech and chiff; less room. | `continuo/partimento.ts`, `continuo/audio.ts`, `continuo/costs.ts` |
| D57 | Live playback: a change of the line, of its versions, or of the continuo while playing restarts the playback at once from the bar under the cursor (the continuo can start part-way through). | `App.tsx`, `ui/playback.ts`, `engine.ts`, `continuo/audio.ts` |
| D58 | Stability (owner): the main score has one fixed layout (it no longer changes height with Intervals, the evaluation or the versions); the versions are drawn on the player's staff in their own inks, named in the legend at the top right; with Intervals on, each version has its own row of intervals against the cantus, italic, in its ink; note names label every line (cantus, yours, Fux's, the versions). The mixer spans the page width and keeps all strips, the four version strips included (dimmed when off, each with its own On/Off); the canon's displacement (‹ C+n ›) is on the Contrapunctus strip and on the Canon strip; Fux's strip shows or hides his solution on the score. The three-voice reading exempts the final two bars (owner). Repeated comments are said once with all their bars (Fux comparison by reason; evaluation by rule, with an excerpt per place; three-voice reading by kind). | `ScoreView.tsx`, `SoundDesk.tsx`, `FuxComparison.tsx`, `Feedback.tsx`, `TrioReading.tsx`, `trio-eval.ts` |
| D59 | Undo/redo of the written line, per exercise (↶ ↷ in the Write group; Ctrl/⌘+Z, Ctrl+Shift+Z, Ctrl+Y). Humanising made clearly audible (stronger accents and phrase arch in loudness and time, more detached repetitions, a steeper velocity response) and switchable beside the view toggles; switching it restarts the playback live. | `App.tsx`, `humanise.ts`, `voice.ts` |
| D60 | Stems on the player's staff are computed, not fixed by voice: lines are ranked by mean pitch; with two lines the higher has stems up and the lower down; with more, the highest up, the lowest down, the others without stems. | `ScoreView.tsx` |
| D61 | Third and fourth species, two voices, from the 1725 text (pp. 63-76, read from the scans). Each course has Fux's six worked exercises (D, E, F; cantus below and above) and then the G, A and C cantus firmi that Aloysius leaves to private study (third, p. 69; fourth, p. 75), as tasks without a solution. Third species: four quarters a bar; the first consonant; a dissonant quarter passes by step in one direction, or is the cambiata; the penultimate bar ends on the note below the final. Fourth species: the half-note layout with Fux's opening rest; a tie is written as the same note on both sides of the bar line (drawn and played tied); the upbeat of a ligature consonant; a dissonant downbeat tied and resolved by step down; no 1-2 / 8-9 (cantus below), no 7-8 (cantus above); cadence 7-6 / 2-3; a ligature where possible (warning). The first-species precepts carry over. Every rule is cleared by Fux's own solutions (D39). The continuo realizes a sung suspension from its resolution, with figures ("7 6", "4 3"), and keeps its right hand off the held dissonance. | `rules/third-species.ts`, `rules/fourth-species.ts`, `curriculum/fux-third-fourth-species.ts`, `layout.ts`, `ScoreView.tsx`, `continuo/realize.ts`, `study.en.json` |
| D62 | Fourth species, "a ligature wherever possible" (p. 74). Aloysius gives two licences for plain minims: necessity ("no room for a ligature") and variety (Josephus leaves out a possible ligature so as not to repeat the ligatures of the third and fourth bars; Aloysius approves on grounds of singing and elegance. Which bars he means depends on how the bar with the rest is counted; unresolved). Necessity is computed: a ligature was possible into bar b when some note, a singable interval from the downbeat of bar b-1, is consonant with that bar's cantus and, held over the bar line, is consonant with the next or a permitted dissonance that can resolve down by step. Every ligature in Fux's six solutions passes this test, and both his omissions (the downbeats of the sixth bar of Fig. 73 and of the fourth of Fig. 75, counting the bar with the rest as the first) were possible ligatures, i.e. both are variety. Variety is not reduced to a rule: Fux ends Fig. 73 with three 7-6s in a row and Fig. 74 with three 2-3s, so "no three alike" would condemn him; he also breaks chains with consonant ligatures rather than omissions. The count is taken from Fux (owner): each exercise allows as many voluntary omissions as his solution makes (1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 0 for Figs. 73-78); the tasks without a solution allow one, the most he takes. Forced omissions never count. | `rules/fourth-species.ts`, `curriculum/fux-third-fourth-species.ts`, `curriculum/index.ts` |
| D63 | Interface pass for denser music. The main score is never drawn below 0.8 of its size: a longer score (third species above all) scrolls sideways inside its box, keeps its scroll across redraws, brings the playback cursor (or else the selection) into view, and pins the clefs at the left edge while scrolled, as a notation editor's continuous view does. Keyboard entry follows the notation editors' convention: a letter writes the note and moves on to the next slot; ↑ ↓ right after a letter correct the note just written, and Shift moves by an octave; T (or +) repeats the previous note, which in fourth species is the tie (a Tie button too). Narrow screens: the page no longer scrolls sideways, and the exercise menu has its own row. The demo area gains third and fourth species (the latter with continuo). | `ScoreView.tsx`, `App.tsx`, `session.ts`, `HelpCard.tsx`, `styles.css`, `demo.ts` |
| D64 | D63's scrolling score withdrawn (owner): the score is again scaled down to fit its box, whatever its length, and never scrolls (smallest scale 0.25, enough for a third-species score on a phone). The pinned clefs go with it. The rest of D63 stays (keyboard entry, the tie key, narrow screens, the demo entries). | `ScoreView.tsx`, `styles.css` |
| D65 | Owner request: the browsing dwell (how long the selection must rest on a note before it sounds) shortened from 0.5 s to 0.15 s. | `App.tsx` |
| D66 | Owner request: an octave stepper (‹ oct 0 ›) on each version strip (Inversion, Retrograde, Retro-inv., Canon), like Fux's: it transposes that line by octaves in playback only (-3..3); the score is unchanged. Stored with the sound settings and in saved pieces. | `sound.ts`, `engine.ts`, `SoundDesk.tsx` |
| D67 | Owner: the Fux comparison no longer lists every place where the two lines differ (the score shows that). It lists only the reasons, from Fux's stated criteria (and Mann's, marked), that favour one choice, each once with its bars; where none applies, it says nothing. | `FuxComparison.tsx` |
| D68 | Owner: once Fux's solution has opened (the exercise cleared, now or earlier: a starred exercise opens it from the start), it stays open. Editing the line, undo, switching the evaluation off or moving to another exercise never hides it: only the player does (Hide on the Fux strip or under the evaluation). | `App.tsx` |
| D69 | Owner: each version strip (Inversion, Retrograde, Retro-inv., Canon) has its own sound. By default it follows the Contrapunctus ("Same sound as Contrapunctus", on); editing a version's synth gives it its own copy and switches that off, and never changes the Contrapunctus. Stored with the sound settings and in saved pieces. | `sound.ts`, `engine.ts`, `SoundDesk.tsx` |
| D70 | Owner: the drum loop length takes the factors 2 and 1.5 both ways (÷2 ÷1.5 [length] ×1.5 ×2), between 1/8 and 8 bars, kept as exact fractions (2/3, 3/2, 9/4 ...); a 2/3-bar loop is a triplet feel against the cantus. Clicking the length returns to one bar. | `drums.ts`, `DrumBox.tsx` |
| D71 | Drum sounds (owner: more presets, vintage analog machines). A Sound row (kit) applies to any pattern: Studio (the game's kit) and synthesized imitations of the Roland TR-808 (1980; bridged-T bass drum near 50 Hz, the six-square-oscillator metal bank, cowbell 540/800 Hz per the service manual), TR-909 (1983; swept analog bass drum, snare with snappy noise; its 6-bit sampled cymbals are imitated, not sampled), CR-78 (1978), Ace Tone Rhythm Ace (1967, the organ-era preset boxes) and Simmons SDS-V (1981; pitch-bent toms). A new family, Drum machines, has seven patterns written for them (Electro and Slow jam on the 808, House and Techno on the 909, New wave on the CR-78, Rhythm box, Synth toms); choosing one selects its machine. New voice: guiro. No samples are used. | `drums.ts`, `DrumBox.tsx` |
| D72 | Owner: with the loop on, the drums never stop and never end on the final crash: the last bar keeps the groove, and the half-bar breath before the next pass (silent before) carries the pattern's fill, or a snare roll, into bar 1; the ending is kept when the loop is off. | `drums.ts`, `engine.ts` |
| D73 | Owner: more continuo ensembles, grouped Baroque (stile antico, cembalo, Hofkapelle, theorbo), Orchestral (pizzicato, sostenuto, brass) and Modern (analog pads, electric piano, rock band). All synthesized: Karplus-Strong strings for theorbo, pizzicato, upright bass and the clean guitar; bowed strings with slow attack; brass as saws through an opening low-pass; a detuned-saw pad with a drifting filter and a resonant mono bass; FM electric piano; the rock band's left hand an electric bass with light drive, its right hand split between an overdriven lead guitar on the top note and a clean chorused rhythm guitar strumming the notes below (owner). Levels matched by offline rendering to about -28 dB RMS. The realization is unchanged; only the sound differs. | `continuo/audio.ts`, `continuo/types.ts`, `continuo-settings.ts`, `ContinuoBox.tsx` |
| D74 | Owner: export of what plays as an audio file. The export (⤓ beside the loop) plays one pass with every current setting and captures the master output (after the limiter) with an AudioWorklet (a ScriptProcessor where blob modules are refused), then encodes it. MP3 at 192 kbps is recommended (about 1.4 MB a minute, plays everywhere, WhatsApp takes it as audio); WAV (16-bit PCM) for lossless editing. With the loop on, the file is exactly one cycle, cut at the sample where the next pass starts, so it loops seamlessly; with it off, the piece, its ending and 2.5 s of reverb tail. Recording runs in real time. Encoder: @breezystack/lamejs 1.2.7 (LGPL-3.0), credited. On claude.ai the file is saved through the viewer's downloads capability, whose allowlist has neither mp3 nor wav, so there it comes inside a stored .zip; elsewhere it is a plain download. | `audio/export.ts`, `engine.ts`, `App.tsx`, `Credits.tsx` |
| D75 | Owner: the export asks how many passes to record (1-16) and how it ends: seamless loop (cut where the next pass would start) or final cadence (the last pass ends with the drums' ending and 2.5 s of tail). Aloysius's "limping crotchets" (pp. 80-81) is a warning from step 11, where he gives it: it is advice, not a precept, and it fires on Fux's own Fig. 88a, bar 5, exactly where he prints the NB. Every other rule is cleared by all twelve of Fux's solutions (D39). Checked at 1000 × 865, 750 × 1000, 550 × 1000, 1366 × 768, 1920 × 1080 and 1280 × 600: nothing scrolls but the text panes. |
| D105 | Owner (D104's scaling made everything tiny in a 40%-of-27" window): native sizes again, in three modes by width. Phone (< 600 px): one column. Tabs (< 1400 px): the score across the top, then the transport and the tabbed dock; when the height leaves room, the text tabs open in a pane under the mixer instead of leaving it dark. Columns (≥ 1400 px): the score across the full width; below it the left column (transport, the strips and the selected strip's editor beneath them) and the right column with the text tabs, which go there by default (⇤ brings one back). The boundary under the score and the one between the columns can be dragged (double-click resets; remembered). The score box is as tall as its music unless dragged. A large screen enlarges everything in proportion from 1600 × 900, at most 1.4 times; the page stops widening at 2000 units. Text panes reflow by their own width (container queries): the guide's two-column grids become one in a narrow pane. The transport buttons smaller. Checked at 550 × 1000, 750 × 1000, 1000 × 865, 1024 × 1100, 1366 × 768, 1600 × 900, 1920 × 1080 and 2560 × 1440: only text panes scroll. |

| D113 | Owner (batch 8: three voices on two staves, voice chips, one fader per voice): the three voices are drawn on a treble and a bass staff joined by a brace, as a keyboard reduction. Each voice goes where its line lies (Fux's line, or the cantus, averaging around middle C or above: treble), not by its 1725 clef, whose alto and tenor C clefs straddle middle C; if all three fall on one staff the lowest takes the other, and a staff whose voices all read in the other clef takes that clef. Each voice has its ink: the cantus black, Contra I and Contra II in their track colours (blue, magenta); two voices a second apart or in unison on a staff stand side by side, and their names part. The figures above the lowest voice stand stacked under the bass staff, as a figured bass. Voice chips (Cantus, Contra I, Contra II, with the voice's place) replace the 'Writing… / Other' line: a chip, Tab, or a tap on a staff chooses the voice written (a tap writes the active voice if it lives on that staff, else the player's voice that does). | D114 | Owner: second species in three voices, after checking the Choices-lab session's findings. Rules from Exercitium II, Lectio II (1725 pp. 94-96, read from the scans): the two-voice second-species precepts for the minim voice against each semibreve (a dissonant upbeat only as a passing note; no perfect consonance in parallel from the upbeat; no repeated minim); the three-voice first-species rules on the downbeats (the minim voice's thesis note, or its first note after the half rest); and Fux's three relaxations: two downbeat fifths or octaves saved by a leap of a third (p. 94; in two voices it took a fourth), the tie into the penultimate bar as a suspension resolving down by step where two minims would give a faulty unison or an empty octave (p. 96), and the major-third final by necessity (p. 96, already admitted in first species). One reading beyond the text, admitted because it is Fux's (D39): at the close a diminished fifth over the bass that resolves inward by step (Fig. 123, where the bass carries the suspension). Direct motion into a perfect consonance is judged from the minim voice's upbeat, not from its downbeat. All nine of Fux's solutions (Figs. 121-129) pass; one warning remains on Fig. 126. The screen: a species selector for three voices (1st, 2nd); the minim voice drawn in half notes with stems (up for the upper voice of a shared staff), its half rest, the cadence tie; a tap on the left or right half of a bar writes the first or second minim; ← → move by one minim; R puts the rest back; playback in half notes, a tie held over the bar line; the continuo harmonises the downbeats. Staves split at middle C (D113 amended: a tenor line averaging just below it went to the treble staff in ledger lines). From the Choices lab, also: the minor sixth is allowed without a direction (p. 60; 'upward' was a later gloss); Fig. 110's bar-6 B (B natural in the dataset, likely B flat by the period's convention) left for the owner to decide. | `three-voice-second.ts`, `trio.ts`, `TrioScore.tsx`, `TrioApp.tsx`, `trio-staves.ts` |
| D115 | Owner: bring in the hint engine and the harmonic view from the Choices lab (the 'Fux counterpoint thesis analysis' session, branch claude/beautiful-mccarthy-7li5nv). Its modules were imported as they stand (src/counterpoint/choices: hints, alternatives, score, corpus, habits, features, keys, vocabulary, harmony); the lab page, generators and studies stay on that branch. Hints (two voices, species 1-4, the range in which the lab checked that Fux's note is legal at every step): a Hint button on the score's toolbar (H) opens a bar under the transport for the note being written: how many pitches the rules allow there given the rest of the line, whether the written one is among them (else the rule it breaks, in the game's own words), its rank among them by Fux's counsel and habits, and on request the most Fux-like note, with a button to write it. The habits are learnt from Fux's other solutions of the species, never from the exercise in hand, so the suggestion is not his own note copied. Computed 90 ms after each change (first species about 10 ms, third species up to 0.7 s at the worst slot). The harmonic view (three voices): 'Harmony (modern)' in the View menu puts a Roman numeral under each bar's figures, relative to the final, in brackets where an incomplete chord leaves the root a guess; labelled a later lens (Rameau, Weber), never graded. | `HintBar.tsx`, `hints-context.ts`, `ScoreTools.tsx`, `App.tsx`, `TrioScore.tsx`, `TrioApp.tsx`, `choices/*` |
| D116 | Owner: third and fourth species in three voices; Fig. 110, bar 6, read B flat and flagged open. Rules from Exercitium II, Lectiones III-IV (1725 pp. 99-111, read from the scans). Third species: a dissonant crotchet only as a passing note or the cambiata, the downbeat consonant, against both semibreves (a fourth or a false fifth with the upper semibreve stands, as in first species); a bar whose crotchets never make the triad is noted (p. 100: 'if the first crotchet cannot make the harmonic triad, see that the second or the third does'). Fourth species: a dissonant downbeat tied from the upbeat and resolving down by step; the preparation consonant except over a held bass, where chains of dissonant ligatures are 'elegantissimè' (p. 107, Josephus's fourth in Fig. 141, bar 3); no ligature from the octave to the ninth or the unison to the second above the bass, none from the seventh to the octave in the bass; a dissonant ligature prepared and resolved on the same perfect consonance hides two fifths or octaves and does not save them (pp. 104-105), tolerated with the moving voice below ('in parte gravi ... tolerantur', p. 105); consonant syncopations are not retardations and are read as written; rests where no ligature is possible (pp. 108, 111); a ligature wherever possible, with as many omissions as Fux's own solution has. Three readings beyond the text, admitted because they are Fux's (D39): the cadential false fifth over the bass resolving inward (Figs. 144, 151; Fig. 123 in second species); the crotchet neighbour note making a tritone at the close (Fig. 132, A-G-A in the bass under C#); unisons with the moving voice in fourth species not noted (they come from the ligatures, Fig. 147). All thirteen of Fux's solutions pass (Figs. 130-133, 141, 143-144, 146-151); Fig. 148 has two triad notes. Fux's rests anywhere in the moving voice are mapped onto its slots (a crotchet rest opens Fig. 132, a whole bar Fig. 144, a minim in Fig. 151). The screen: species 1-4 in three voices; the moving voice in two or four notes a bar (wider bars for crotchets), ties drawn across the bar line, a rest anywhere (R); a tap on the quarter or half of the bar writes that note. Not modelled: the hidden fifths between alto and cantus at the opening that Aloysius would avoid with a rest (p. 111), a counsel rather than a rule. | `three-voice-florid.ts`, `trio.ts`, `TrioScore.tsx`, `TrioApp.tsx`, `open-questions.md` |
| D117 | Fifth species in three voices (Exercitium II, Lectio V, 1725 pp. 111-114, read from the scans). Fux gives no new rule: florid counterpoint is 'the composition of all five species, ordered by a beautiful and elegant manner of singing', as said in two voices, and the concords of the two semibreve parts are known 'from the three-voice exercises worked through so far', so that it would be superfluous to dwell on them (pp. 111-112); after six examples, oblique motion in every bar, as much as possible, makes the labour much easier (p. 114, a counsel, not graded). Reading: the two-voice florid rules (D82: downbeat consonance, suspensions with their forbidden kinds, weak-beat dissonance as passing note, cambiata or quaver neighbour, quavers on the weak crotchets, ligatures, limping) for the florid voice against the lower semibreve of each bar (the bass, or the partner above when the florid voice is the bass); against the upper semibreve, only the true dissonances (a fourth or a false fifth between upper voices stands, as in first species); the three-voice first-species rules on the sonority each bar opens with (the florid note at the downbeat, held or struck, or the note a tied dissonance resolves to), and among the semibreves; perfect consonances in a row across the bar line between the florid voice and either semibreve; the melodic leaps of the florid line; the close by a semitone in some voice. One reading beyond the text, admitted because it is Fux's (D39) and already admitted in species 2-4 (D114, D116): the cadential false fifth with the upper voice, the florid bass's suspended fourth resolving into it (Fig. 156, bar 10: D tied under G, to C# under G, then D under F#). All six of Fux's solutions pass (Figs. 154-159), with no notes. The screen: species 5 in three voices; the florid voice in quaver slots with held slots as in two voices (note values on keys 8, 4, 3, 2, 6, 1; T or Hold carries the previous note on, over the bar line as a ligature; R a rest anywhere; the opening half rest given), drawn with flags, dots and ties, Fux's notes as diamonds at their own places in the bar; playback from each onset for its held length. | `three-voice-fifth.ts`, `trio.ts`, `TrioScore.tsx`, `TrioApp.tsx`, `en.json` |
| D118 | D8 amended (owner's approval, 9 October 2026, from the Choices lab), the cantus firmus generator's constraints checked against Fux's own cantus firmi under D39: length 9-14 accepted (Fux's C variant has 9 notes; the generator still writes 10-14 unless asked); a sixth outlined by two leaps the same way allowed when they arpeggiate a triad (Fux's G: G-C-E); the turn after a rising fourth or more made soft, counted not forbidden (Fux's G does not turn); leaps 20-62% of the intervals (Fig. 42's A has 20%, the G 62%); new: the antepenultimate note on degree 1 or 3 (true of all Fux's cantus firmi, Ewing 2009); constraint 15 (every cantus admits a counterpoint) checked when the counterpoint is generated, not as a filter on the cantus, the least bad counterpoint shown in red where none is error-free. Unchanged: 1, 2, 4-9, 12, 13. Nothing in the game depends on them yet. Full table: docs/fux/cantus-constraints-amendment.md on branch claude/beautiful-mccarthy-7li5nv. | `cantus-constraints-amendment.md` (lab branch) |
| D119 | Owner: "the end goal of this whole thing is to have a Well-Tempered Clavier mode"; the brief's Stage III (WTC material, fugal writing). A third mode beside two and three voices: **Well-Tempered Clavier**. Material: 29 fugue expositions (Book I: 14, Book II: 15; 21 of the 24 keys; C minor, E♭ minor and E minor are missing from the source) extracted from the ASAP dataset's MusicXML (Foscarin et al., ISMIR 2020; CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, attribution and licence in `data/bach/wtc/README.md`) by `tools/wtc-extract.py`: the encodings' voice numbers are unreliable, so the lines are found from the notes (one line until two notes sound, two until three do, separated by register; written-out trills beside their main note dropped); the subject lasts as long as the answer repeats it at a fixed transposition, mutations at the head (the first five notes) aside; the countersubject is what the dux sings over the answer. Checked: every answer is the subject a fifth up or a fourth down but for its head mutations, which are all the textbook ones (the dominant answered by the tonic, or the tonic by the dominant; Book I's B major keeps four notes of its head in the tonic). Three exercises per fugue. (1) **The answer**: the player writes the comes in the subject's rhythm; Bach's answer is the solution (Bach is the last word, as Fux in D39); verdicts name a real answer where Bach's is tonal, with the degrees ('the subject's 5 is answered by 1, not by 2'), a mutation where Bach's is real, a spelling, or a note that does not follow the subject's interval; a whole answer an octave away is right. (2) **The countersubject**: the player writes the dux's line against Bach's answer, in Bach's rhythm, judged by a two-voice tonal engine: the species' dissonance doctrine carried into free rhythm and the keys, by metric strength (the beat from the time signature: the denominator, three of them in compound time). On the beat a dissonance is a passing note or neighbour, a suspension (held or struck again, resolving down by step), or stands against the answer's own passing note or suspension when consonant with its main note; off the beat it is entered or left by step (incomplete neighbour, échappée, cambiata), anticipates, belongs to changing notes, or belongs to a chord the two voices imply with a neighbouring note (triads, dominant, minor, major, half-diminished and diminished sevenths); a tritone resolving to a third or sixth (also after one skip) stands; a held note made dissonant on the beat resolves down by step; no two fifths or octaves in a row. The appoggiatura, which Bach's countersubjects here never use, is a fault named as such. Calibration: all 29 of Bach's lines pass; of 189 dissonances injected on the beat into Bach's lines, 115 are caught (most of the others are legitimate against the answer's passing notes); off the beat the leniency is by design. (3) **Study**: Bach's exposition with the parts named and the mutations marked ✱. The score (`WtcScore`) is VexFlow's formatter on a grand staff (one staff where both voices share it, as Bach writes them): key signature, accidentals by the bar, beams by the metre, dots, triplets, ties over the bar line; the player's notes are grey slots in the given rhythm; letters write with the key signature, in the answer at the octave that follows the subject's contour. Sound: harpsichord (default), organ or piano; temperaments Werckmeister III (default), Kirnberger III, Vallotti, or equal; Kirnberger III and Vallotti are computed from the sizes of their fifths, a function that reproduces the existing Werckmeister III table exactly. The Keys tab lays out the 24 keys in Bach's order with each book's fugue. Fux's own fugue chapters (after the species in four voices) are not among the scans, so the bridge text paraphrases the doctrine without quoting him. | `wtc/*`, `WtcApp.tsx`, `WtcScore.tsx`, `temperament.ts`, `Root.tsx`, `tools/wtc-extract.py`, `data/bach/wtc/*` |
| D120 | WTC mode, two additions. (1) **Real or tonal?**, now the first exercise of each fugue: the player marks the subject's notes that a tonal answer changes (or none: a real answer); the verdict names each with its degrees ('note 1, degree 5: Bach answers F, degree 1, not G, 2'); the theory decision behind the comes, made before writing it. (2) **The hint** in the countersubject (H, after D115): the notes the rules allow at the selected place given the notes written around it. Candidates: every step within a seventh of the note before, on the key's degrees (the scale and the leading tones of its other degrees: in major ♯1 ♯2 ♯4 ♯5 ♭7, in minor the raised 6th and 7th, ♯3, ♯4 and the Neapolitan ♭2); tested: each of Bach's notes within a seventh of the one before is among those allowed. | `WtcApp.tsx`, `wtc/hints.ts` |
| D121 | WTC mode: **the whole fugue**. Every note of the 29 fugues (31,795, `data/bach/wtc/fugues-full.json`, in 96ths of a quarter, from the same ASAP encodings and cleaning as D119), heard in the chosen instrument and temperament, and drawn as a roll (study exercise: Exposition / Whole fugue, W) with the bar lines and every entry of the subject in colour, upside down in another; a tap on an entry's S plays from there. The entries are found automatically (`wtc/entries.ts`): the subject's rhythm exactly (each note at its onset and of its length, the last free), its intervals at one transposition after the head (from the sixth note, or the fourth of a short subject), the head's notes allowed two semitones off (the tonal mutations), one note at most missing; one entry per onset, the best match; inversions the same way; only the first ten notes of a long subject matched. Tested: each fugue's subject and answer are found where the exposition data puts them. Limits: entries with altered rhythm (augmentation, diminution, a changed head in stretto) are missed, so the counts are a lower bound (C major I: 19 found, against the 24 usually counted). | `wtc/entries.ts`, `PianoRoll.tsx`, `WtcApp.tsx`, `tools/wtc-extract.py` |
| D122 | WTC mode, the well temperament made audible and a fourth exercise. (1) **Find the entries**: the whole fugue as a roll without colours; the player marks the first notes of the subject's entries and is scored against the machine's entries (D121): four in five for the star; a mark that matches none is reported, not counted against, since the machine finds only entries in the subject's exact rhythm. (2) **The tonic's third**: each fugue states the size, in the chosen temperament, of its tonic's major or minor third, beside equal and pure (Werckmeister III: C–E 390.2 cents, F♯–A♯ 407.8; Kirnberger III: C–E pure, 386.3; Vallotti: C–E 392.2), so that the colour of a key is a measured fact. (3) **Equal ↔ well**: the exposition's opening twice, in equal temperament and then in the chosen well temperament (Werckmeister III if equal is chosen); the engine reads the temperament as each note starts, so the switch falls between the passes. (4) **A course order** of the fugues (Keys tab), beside Bach's: by a difficulty score (the subject's length, eight for each mutated note, half the countersubject's length), short subjects and real answers first. | `WtcApp.tsx`, `PianoRoll.tsx` |
| D123 | Owner: the WTC mode is above all a study ("the most deep and insightful interactions with this incredible timeless piece of work"; exercises "the least of our concern, for starters", though game ideas stay welcome: "write the response"). The mode now opens on **the study**; the exercises (D119-D122) are one button away. For each of the 29 fugues, whole: (1) **the voice roll**, every note in its voice's colour, the subject's entries outlined (S, ∀ upside down), a tap plays from that bar; (2) **voices**, found from the notes (`wtc/voices.ts`: as many lines as the polyphony that holds for all but 5% of the piece; at each onset the new notes go, in order, to the free lines at least cost; each entry's notes kept in one line): Solo, Mute, and **Spotlight** (that voice in another instrument, the others at 40%), each voice on its own channel and place left to right; tested: overlaps within a voice 49 of 31,795 notes, more than 97% of entries in one voice; (3) **the guide** (`wtc/study.ts`): sections (exposition: the first entries, one per voice; then entries and episodes in turn; the close) and moments (each entry with its voice and degree, strettos, episodes, pedal points, the highest and lowest notes, the final cadence), each to hear, an entry also alone; every entry in a row, alone or in its texture; (4) **the workshop**: change the subject's notes (a step in the key or a semitone) and hear it through Bach's whole plan, every entry at his places and pitches (upside down where his is), alone (the fugue's architecture with your subject) or inside his unchanged texture; add entries of your own (bar, degree, upside down) against his fugue; (5) **Where next?**: the fugue unfolds entry by entry; before each, the player names the degree the subject has moved to; the entry is revealed and played, the score kept. Everything is found by the game from the notes and says so. The lab session's 48-piece Humdrum corpus (true voices, all preludes) could not be copied in (refused by the session's permission classifier); it awaits the owner. | `WtcStudy.tsx`, `VoiceRoll.tsx`, `wtc/voices.ts`, `wtc/study.ts`, `Root.tsx` |
| D124 | WTC study, two additions, while the import of the lab session's 48-piece corpus awaits the owner (copying it in was refused twice by the session's permission check, the second time after the owner's "just go"; not worked around). (1) **Score view**: the chosen section or moment in notation, voice by voice in its colour, at most eight bars (with nothing chosen, pages of four bars that turn as it plays), each entry marked S or ∀; the machine's MIDI spelled in the key (the step the key signature gives, else a natural, else a sharp, a flat in a flat key). (2) **Write the response** (the owner's game idea): in "Where next?", after naming the degree the player writes the entry's first four notes by letter (key signature applied, ♯ ♭ alter the last), checked by pitch class against Bach's, then hears the entry; two scores kept (degrees, notes). | `WtcStudy.tsx` |
| D125 | WTC study: **the preludes** and **a harmonic reading**, while the lab corpus awaits the owner. (1) The 29 preludes that go with the 29 fugues, from the same ASAP scores and licence (`tools/wtc-preludes.py`, the fugue extractor's pooling and ornament filter, `data/bach/wtc/preludes-full.json`); a Prelude/Fugue switch at the top; a prelude has the roll, the score view, the strands (Voices: where a figuration holds a note while it moves on, the held note is a strand), its pedal points, highest and lowest notes and close, and sections by the keys it reaches (else by its changes of figuration); the workshop and Where next? are the fugue's. (2) **Harmony** (`wtc/harmony.ts`), for preludes and fugues: each bar, split in halves (twice at most, never below a beat) where two chords fit much better than one, gets the triad or seventh chord whose tones carry most of its sounding time (a held note counting no more than a beat, a note on the beat more than one between beats; a missing tone costs; the bass breaks ties; a seventh only if it sounds a tenth of the time; a line alone gets none); a diminished seventh takes its root from the chord it resolves to. Named by letter (Dm7/C) or Roman numeral in the piece's key with inversion figures, a chord outside the key named by the degree it leads to (V⁴₂/V, vii°⁴₃/ii), the leading-tone chord in minor plain vii°, the final major chord in minor I. Cadences: a root-position dominant to a root-position tonic a fifth below; the keys reached in turn ("the keys, by their cadences"). Shown as a strip of chords over the roll and a list by bar, each to hear; **the chords alone** play the skeleton as blocks (bass, tones above middle C). Tested on the C major prelude (bars 1–15 as the textbooks read them; keys G at bar 11, C at bar 19). Honest about its limits in the guide: in contrapuntal textures passing and suspended notes can be read as chord tones. | `wtc/harmony.ts`, `tools/wtc-preludes.py`, `WtcStudy.tsx`, `VoiceRoll.tsx` |

| D140 | Owner: a TUTORIAL for learners with no musical knowledge, taking them to the end of the game, click-based. Owner's choices (9 October, night): lessons on a screen of their own plus a spotlight tour of the real screen; four voices as a short read-only preview (the game has no four-voice screen, and our scans stop at p. 90); three voices hands-on in first species. **Shape:** TUTORIAL (teal, beside HOW TO PLAY) opens a screen over the game, which stays mounted, hidden and silent, so nothing written is lost. 13 chapters, 51 lessons: welcome; reading notes (staff, letters, octave, steps and leaps, sharps); intervals (counting, hearing, perfect / imperfect / dissonant); motion (the four kinds, a quiz, the rule of perfect consonances); the cantus firmus, the modes, the five species; one chapter per species; three voices; four voices (preview); the road map of the game (exercise counts and stars) and the tour. Each lesson: text, one task (read, listen, quiz, choice, write, judged writing, three-voice writing, open in the game, tour), Next once done; Hint, Show me (Fux's notes), Start again, Skip; progress remembered (`wtg.tutorial`). A first visit (no tutorial, no star) is invited once (`wtg.tutorialInvite`). **Material:** Fux's own lines only. The first exercise of each species is on the same D cantus (Figs. 5, 33, 55, 73, 82; Fig. 101 in three voices), so the learner follows one melody through the book. Judged tasks leave out a few of Fux's notes and judge the learner's with the rules of that exercise in the game (`rulesForStep`); quiz answers are computed from the notes; the fifth-species choice is decided by the engine. Aloysius's lines are the verified ones of `study.en.json`. A coach under the score names each note's interval and the motion into it (only once intervals are taught). From the game back: every broken rule in the evaluation (two and three voices) offers "Learn this in the tutorial ›", opening the lesson that teaches it (`tutorial/links.ts`; every rule has one). A first-species lesson on the line itself (tritone and major-sixth leaps, unisons, converging leaps into the octave). The tour shows the learner's own two-voice exercise; nothing is moved. **Tests:** every answer passes, every wrong option breaks the rule its text names, the hints' alternatives (B♮ for Fux's B♭) pass, quiz answers checked by hand. | `src/tutorial/`, `src/content/tutorial.en.json`, `ui/Tutorial.tsx`, `ui/TutorialParts.tsx`, `ui/Tour.tsx`, `ui/Root.tsx`, `App.tsx`, `TrioApp.tsx`, `HeaderTools.tsx`, `test/tutorial.test.ts` |

| D132 | **Bach as the last word, laxly** (the owner: Bach is the golden standard but also broke rules, "so, Bach is the last word should mean something laxer"). The countersubject checker stays strict (its tests unchanged); `tools/wtc/licences.ts` runs it on Bach's own fugues, each upper voice against the bass, keeping only moments where exactly two voices sound (like the exercise; in fuller textures a dissonance against the bass is often a chord tone or a pedal, which a two-voice checker cannot see): 16 kinds of fault, about 60 places (`data/wtc/licences.json`): octave doublings (E minor I, bars 19 and 38; C♯ major II, bar 14), a fourth on the beat over the bass (11 places in 8 fugues), sevenths left unresolved or leapt into, one parallel fifth (C♯ major I, bar 39). In the exercise such a fault is a warning, a licence naming where Bach takes it ("Bach does this too … for instance Book II, Fugue 1 in C major, bar 78"); everything else stays an error. Note-against-note parallel fifths or octaves are otherwise absent from the 48 fugues. — **Entries checked** (the owner: "Ledbetter was supposed to settle all this"): Ledbetter's commentary (Yale 2002) is discursive, with no table of entries, and neither it nor Bruhn's analyses (1993) are available here; so the entries are checked for what must hold: the exposition brings in every voice, entries in one voice do not overlap. Overlaps (A major I, a subject of even quavers matched a few notes on) are now removed, the lab's finder first; left: C minor II (Bach's exposition is in three of its four voices), C♯ major II (its third entry is inverted), C♯ minor I (the second soprano's entry at bar 12 alters the head and is missed). | `wtc/licences.ts`, `tools/wtc/licences.ts`, `WtcApp.tsx`, `wtc/library.ts` |
| D133 | **The companion** (the owner: "very important … the impression that he's listening to this great music alongside somebody else who is very knowledgeable but also a lover of this music"). `wtc/commentary.ts`: for every moment, a few sentences on what is happening and why it matters, built only from what the analysis knows: the first statement ("worth humming once; you will hear it n more times"), the answer, the exposition's completion, the first entry in a new key region (relative major, subdominant…), inversions, the bass carrying the subject, strettos (and late strettos raising the tension), links within the exposition (codettas) and episodes (varied texts, the key regions between which they move), tonic and dominant pedals and what each does, the highest and lowest notes (whether the summit falls inside an entry), the close (the Picardy third, read from the last chord). An opening paragraph per piece from the same facts (voices, bars, the subject's length and number of entries, inversions and strettos, the key regions visited beyond tonic and dominant). `wtc/notes.ts`: hand-written notes on the character of 14 pieces, limited to what is well established (the C major prelude's single figure and Gounod; the C♯ minor five-voice fugue; the E minor two-voice fugue and its octaves; the B minor fugue's twelve-note subject; E major II and Fischer; F♯ minor II's three subjects …); no note is better than an unsure one. **Listening with you**: while a piece plays (game sounds or a recording), a line over the score says what is sounding now (stretto before entry before pedal …); a checkbox in the guide turns it off. | `wtc/commentary.ts`, `wtc/notes.ts`, `WtcStudy.tsx`, `styles.css` |
| D134 | **The Page view as a score** (the owner: "whatever it takes to have a good Gerubach-style score"). (1) Voices change staff as an engraver would: the highest voice always on the treble staff, the lowest on the bass, a middle voice on the staff its register needs bar by bar, switching only when it clearly leaves the other (its mean above D4 or below A3), so a five-voice fugue's tenor moves down and its alto up as they travel. (2) Chords within a voice drawn as chords, with their accidentals (notes of a different length struck with them are left out). (3) Stems: two voices on a staff, the upper's up and the lower's down. (4) Two systems to a page at least (scaled to the page's height), so the eye reads ahead as in a printed score; as many bars to a system as fill its width, from the music's own density (two bars of a slow 4/2, eight of quick semiquavers). The Score view shares (1) and (2). | `WtcStudy.tsx`, `WtcScore.tsx` |

Brought in from the lab branch (claude/beautiful-mccarthy-7li5nv, 78143cd) only `data/wtc/ledbetter-claims.txt` (491 bar-number claims from the per-piece digest, each read in the encoded score on claude/vibrant-gates-fe6o3h, corrected claims carrying the score's reading) and the lab's summary `docs/wtc/ledbetter-check.md`; from the digest branch (7ea4e35) `docs/wtc/ledbetter/claims-score-check.md`. `tools/wtc/ledbetter-game.ts` runs the claims against what the study screen computes (entries of `library.ts`, strettos, pedals and sections of `study.ts`, keys reached of `harmony.ts`) and writes `docs/wtc/ledbetter-game-check.md`. Fugues: entries 88% (the lab's analysis 90%), strettos 74% (77%), pedals 25% (58%), keys reached on his cadences 23% (62%), section starts 76%; preludes: pedals 3 of 3, cadences 14%, sections 20%. Where they disagree: (1) the study calls any overlap of entries a stretto, so long-subject expositions read as strettos (Book I nos. 4, 17, 19, 22 at b. 1; Book II no. 9 at bb. 1–4), which the lab fixed (the next voice within half the subject); (2) pedals only in the bass and of two bars or more, so his short and inner pedals are missed (Book I no. 1 bb. 19, 21–2; Book II no. 21's alto and soprano pedals); (3) later subjects of double and triple fugues are not sought (I/4, II/4, II/23 …; the lab's `subjects.ts`), nor Book I no. 4's final S1 stretti from b. 94; (4) the harmony shows only changes of key, and misses most of his cadences; (5) sections are the game's (entry groups and episodes), not his (the lab's `ledbetter-sections.json` has his for 32 fugues). The hand notes (`notes.ts`) agree with the digest except in emphasis: I/5 (he: not literally a French overture, its features taken into a fugue), I/8 prelude (the lute is ours; he: a tombeau's character, florid lines over repeated chords), I/24 fugue (he warns against too slow a Largo), II/9 (the subject is archetypal; the model is Fischer's E major fugue in its layout and cadences). | D137 | The owner (on D136's report: "items 1, 2, 3 and 5"): the study corrected by Ledbetter's claims and the lab's rules. (1) **Strettos**: an entry overlapping the one before is a stretto only if it begins before the one before is halfway through the subject (the lab's rule); the false strettos in long-subject expositions are gone (Book I nos. 4, 17, 19, 22; Book II no. 9). (2) **Pedal points** in a fugue (the lab's rule): the lowest sounding note held, or struck again after rests of a beat at most, for a bar and four beats at least; above the bass, the tonic or dominant held for two bars and eight beats (an inner or upper pedal, named as such by the guide and the companion); 18 pedals in the 48 fugues become 50, 6 of them above the bass. (3) **Later subjects** of double and triple fugues (Book I no. 4; Book II nos. 4, 9, 14, 18, 23): the lab's `subjects.ts` and `later-subjects.json` (where each enters is Ledbetter's; its shape and other entries are found in the notes), with the lab's entry finder (`fugue.ts`: a long subject's varied opening, the closing notes' lengths free in short subjects, which also finds Book I no. 4's final stretti from b. 94); occurrences carried on in sequence joined into one statement, none overlapping in a voice. Augmentation and diminution (also in the lab's finder) are not shown. Its 32 four-voice exercises (Figs. 160–204: first species on D, E, F with the cantus in each of the four parts, Figs. 160–172; second, third, fourth and fifth on D, with third also on E; Fig. 204, second species, p. 138) are encoded in Humdrum, each with its figure, species, mode, cantus part, clefs and printed page, CC0. | D148 | Owner (four voices, after D139, D147): the Fux mode in four voices, Exercitium III (1725 pp. 114-138, read from the print), as Josephus does it. **Data**: Part III vendored from the same dataset commit (32 exercises, Figs. 160-204; upstream `gap_201.json` has a trailing comma, read without it); `tools/fux_import/build_four_voice.py` writes the full file and a compact one for the game (run-length quaver slots, 12.6 KB: the artifact stands at 16.67 MB). **Exercises** (120, in the REAL chain after the three-voice ones): per species Fux's own, then the modes he leaves to private study, four a mode, the parts placed as in his D set of that species (owner): first species G, A, C (p. 121); second E, F, G, A, C (p. 123); third F, G, A, C (pp. 128, 131); fourth and fifth E, F, G, A, C (pp. 136, 138); last the species combined, Fig. 204 alone (owner: nothing beyond the print; p. 139 is outside the vendored pages; first species E with the cantus in the bass, promised p. 118 but not printed, not added). Fux's cantus firmi, in each part's octave as he places D, E, F. **Rules** (owner's choices marked): the three-voice evaluators generalised to every pair of four parts; the fourth between inner parts stands (pp. 117, 134); the unison is not noted, being the octave for doubling (p. 114, owner); fifths and octaves reached by similar motion stay a warning, tolerated by necessity (p. 115, pp. 125-131: Fux's Figs. 167-170, 173, 176 then miss the gold, owner); doubling, spacing and the full triad on the thesis (pp. 114, 116-118, 126, 135) are counsel, not graded (owner). Species 2-3: the penultimate bar may be one semibreve (p. 123; Figs. 173-176, 181), and may repeat into the final; the semibreves stay whole (p. 125). Species 4-5: a semibreve may be divided into two minims where necessity asks (pp. 132-133, 138; Figs. 195, 197, 202, 203), unmarked (owner); the moving part is judged against the half that sounds with it. Note values are checked (q.values). **Readings beyond the text, to confirm**: (1) a divided semibreve's second minim is judged as a second-species upbeat (consonant or passing; no perfect consonances in a row from it); (2) at the close the crotchet neighbour note is admitted whatever it meets (Fig. 182, bar 10, A-G-A in the bass a second under the tenor's A; D116 admitted it only as a tritone); (3) the combined species: each moving part judged by its own species against the others as they sound, a note dissonant with the part below it all (a suspension, a passing crotchet) heard by the others as that lowest note, being its own part's affair; (4) a suspension against a divided semibreve is re-judged at each moment it sounds. All 32 of Fux's solutions pass; each of his parts can be re-entered on the screen. **Screen** (`QuartetApp`, on the three-voice pattern): four staves' worth on two (the two highest parts above), voice chips, a third mixer channel (Contra III, `third`), species 1st-5th and Combined, a tap writes the part's value (semibreve, minim, crotchet; florid as chosen), Hold (T) carries a note on (a ligature, a penultimate semibreve), a tap on the right half divides a semibreve (species 4-5); private-study tasks say so and have no Fux strip. | `four-voice.ts`, `three-voice*.ts`, `game/quartet.ts`, `game/unlock.ts`, `QuartetApp.tsx`, `TrioScore.tsx`, `trio-staves.ts`, `sound.ts`, `engine.ts`, `SoundDesk.tsx`, `styles.ts`, `build_four_voice.py`, `test/four-voice.test.ts` |

A step wants at least 2.5% the right way; a repeat wants the thumb within 9%; between the two either will do; only a clear move the wrong way fails. With the anchor in the top or bottom 12%, a step towards that edge only needs not to move clearly away. A held thumb sliding 3% or more from the anchor plays the next note if the line moves that way (a slur; repeats are re-tapped). Lanes stay for NOTES only. Measured with a scripted player on the alto at ♩=60: following the line roughly (30-60 px steps, ±15 px on repeats) 97-100%; random movement 41%; a still thumb 13%. | D166 | Owner: "there is simply no indication of where to tap; how can a person guess by the vertical axis? Make it much easier for a human; iPhone support at all times; on the iPhone it does not even work." **Why it failed on the iPhone**: the game started its sound (and so its clock, which is the sound's) on the touch going down; Safari starts a page's sound only on a finished tap (the lift), so the context stayed suspended and the game stood at the count-in. Now the game starts on the lift, waits for the sound to be running before it begins, and every finished tap, click or key anywhere unlocks the sound; on an iPhone a silent looping <audio> moves the sound to the media channel, so the ring/silent switch does not mute it. **Why it was impossible**: the notes were drawn in the top half and judged against nothing visible under the thumbs. **The notes now come to the thumbs**: each note to tap is a square in the play area, in its voice's colour with its name, and a frame closes in on it, meeting it at the onset (the approach of osu!, Cytus); a long note carries a HOLD bar that fills while held; a dotted line leads to the next square; a hit bursts, a miss leaves a red cross, the grade is written beside the tap. With one voice the squares alternate between the halves (left thumb, right thumb); with two, the lower voice's are on the left, the higher's on the right. A square's height follows the melody, mapped over the range of its bar and the next. **Two levels** replace rhythm, shape and notes: EASY (a tap anywhere on time; ±250 ms; notes shorter than an eighth that follow a tapped note within a beat play by themselves, drawn as dots on the path, and do not score) and NORMAL (every note a square; a tap must land within 70 px of its centre; ±200 ms). The computer's keys play EASY; NORMAL takes clicks. Default tempo ♩=48. Checked: the audit (every voice of every section earns at both levels with a simulated thumb landing within ~20 px); an emulated iPhone 13 with the browser's own sound rules: one tap starts, a scripted thumb tapping the squares earns the alto in NORMAL 75/75. | `src/touch/targets.ts`, `paint-targets.ts`, `engine.ts`, `lanes.ts`, `PlayArea.tsx`, `TouchApp.tsx`, `chip.ts`, `touch.css`, `tools/touch/audit.ts`, `docs/touch/AUDIT.md`, `test/touch-thumbs.test.ts` |
| D167 | Owner: a mode where one plays a single voice accompanied by the others; all the fugues; the squares' sideways movement looked random and kept the eyes off the music above; better control of the instrument, learning from the state of the art of playing instruments on a phone; a little better sound. **All 48 fugues** (`data/touch/fugues.json`, 1.4 MB, from the study's Humdrum corpus): voices named by count (upper/lower; S A B; S A T B; S I, S II, A T B), the subject's entries from the study, an entry's key the subject's moved by its transposition with its mode read from the third above its tonic in its notes, spelled as the piece's key signature spells (the C minor fugue keeps its hand-read keys and sections); sections cut automatically: the exposition (until every voice has entered), then 4 to 10 bars each ending where an entry lands. The beat is the felt one (a dotted quarter in 6/8, a dotted eighth in 12/16), the tempo given for it (40, 50, 60, 72). **The keys** replace the squares: the play area is crossed by rows, one per staff step (a line or a space: a row plays the score's B or B♭ alike), lowest at the bottom, labelled with letter and octave, the rows on the treble and bass staff lines carrying a line; the music box draws the voice on the same rows, so a note's height above is its row below and the eyes can stay on the music. A voice whose range in a section fits 12 rows keeps its rows; a wider one has them set afresh every two bars (at most 14; 0.11% of all notes, in wide leaps, fall beyond and are played on the edge row). The next notes light their rows in the voice's colour (the light-up keys of piano tutors), the nearest with a bar closing in from both ends to meet on the beat; HOLD notes fill their row as they last. NORMAL: a tap on the note's row or the next; holding, a slide to another row plays the next note (a slur), a sideways wobble bends the note up to 30 cents (vibrato; ThumbJam, GeoShred). EASY unchanged (any tap; quick notes ride). Two voices: each half has its own voice's rows. **WITH OTHERS** (the default): the other voices of the section play by themselves, quieter and placed apart, from the section's start, so a late voice enters into the music; they are thin lines behind the played voice in the box. **WAIT** (practice, after Synthesia): the music stands at each note until it is played; no record. **Sound**: an attack, a short fall to the sustain and a release; a gentle delayed vibrato on long notes; the voices panned low left to high right; a soft low-pass and a compressor; a small generated room; timbres by role (the top voice a 12.5% pulse, inner voices 25% and 50% pulses, the bass a triangle); a quieter metronome. The key colours cover all 24 keys (the circle of fifths round the hue wheel, minor darker). Checked: the audit (2962 runs, every voice of every section of the 48 at both levels, none under 80%); the tests (16 for the touch game); an emulated iPhone 13 with the browser's own rules for sound: one tap starts, a scripted thumb on the rows earned the alto of the C minor exposition in NORMAL with the others playing (75/75); the C♯ minor fugue (five voices, tenor and bass at once) and the G major fugue (6/8, WAIT) shown. | `data/touch/fugues.json`, `tools/touch/extract.ts`, `src/touch/fugue.ts`, `rows.ts`, `paint-keys.ts`, `engine.ts`, `voice-mode.ts`, `chip.ts`, `MusicBox.tsx`, `PlayArea.tsx`, `TouchApp.tsx`, `MapView.tsx`, `lanes.ts`, `touch.css`, `tools/touch/audit.ts`, `docs/touch/AUDIT.md`, `test/touch-thumbs.test.ts` |

| D210 | Owner: "the game is crashing on iPhone (audio starts failing and hissing and then the game slows down and crashes)". **Cause**: nothing the sound made was ever disconnected. Each note builds 3 to 15 Web Audio nodes (oscillators, their gains, a filter, a panner, the vibrato's LFO, breath noise; a drum hit 3 to 10) and chip.ts let them all stay wired into the mix after the note ended (one `disconnect()` in the whole file). Chrome drops such finished branches by itself; Safari keeps them until its garbage collector happens to run, and its audio thread goes on processing them. Measured on a fake context (`tools/audio-load.ts`, the bench with the band at ×4): 50 to 90 nodes made a second, so by the end of I/2 (98 s) 4,800 to 8,500 nodes were still wired, and 32,000 to 46,000 by the end of Contrapunctus XIV; the audio thread overruns (crackle, hiss), the page slows and iOS kills it. The music itself is light: at most 10 notes (about 25 sources) sound at once in either fugue, and the bench schedules only 0.25 s ahead. **Fix** (chip.ts): every sound's nodes are gathered (`Bag`) and disconnected together when the last of its sources has ended (notes, recorded notes and their key-up noise, drums, ticks, the swoosh): at most 60 to 130 nodes wired at once, none left after a fugue. And a cap on notes sounding at once (`VOICE_CAP`: 24 on a touch screen, 64 on a computer): past it the oldest note (never the player's own) is let go quickly as the new one starts; the fugues never reach it (insurance for dense ornaments, riders, ORGANO PLENO). The sound, the music, the scheduling and the gameplay unchanged. Checked and left: the noise buffer and the room are built once; one AudioContext per page; the game's lists are bounded (band, marks, pops, flames, bursts, riders); the play area's canvas already caps its pixel ratio at 2 and the music box draws at low resolution; the harpsichord's recordings take about 51 MB on a phone (folded to mono over the 96 MB budget). Not yet tried on an iPhone. | `src/touch/chip.ts` (`Bag`, `VOICE_CAP`, `isPhone`, `makeRoom`, `sounding`); `test/fake-audio.ts`, `test/audio-load.test.ts`; `tools/audio-load.ts` |
| D211 | Owner: "on MOBILE the fugues are not sounding correctly … as if some voices are cancelling the others … almost as if played on a monophonic instrument". Not reproducible in Chromium (no WebKit here). The mechanism found: D210's cap counted a note as sounding until its nodes reported their end (`onended`); where that report comes late or never, the count filled the cap and every new note cut the oldest still sounding, a held note of another voice (a fake context with the reports silenced: 376 of 400 notes cut). Now a note counts only to its written end (and 0.4 s of tail, or its stop), entries long past are forgotten, the phone's cap is 32; every source's stop goes through `halt` (never throwing: older WebKit threw on a second stop); `note()` never throws into the scheduler (`failed` counts); a phone schedules 0.5 s ahead. Not yet confirmed on an iPhone. Also: COMPUTER or MOBILE asked every time the game opens (not remembered); LADDER's box draws the voices beyond the player's range squeezed into its top and bottom margins (`spill`), the rows (lined up with the bars) unchanged. | `src/touch/chip.ts` (`halt`, `Live.until`, `makeRoom`, `failed`), `proto-game.ts` (horizon), `BenchApp.tsx` (platform, `spill`), `MusicBox.tsx` (`spill`, `hOf`), `box-fx.ts`; `test/audio-load.test.ts` |
