| D213 | Owner (experiment E1, design approved): **the subject as protagonist, by ear, not by loudness**; and "a sound effect on the subject, subtle but nice, tailored per fugue". **Default on**: while a voice states the subject (any entry, subject or answer, every theme; not the countersubjects), its notes take a high shelf of +3 dB (from about four times the note's pitch, 1.2-3.5 kHz) and a crisper attack (the shelf lifted 6 dB more for the first 50 ms; a synthesized patch's attack halved and its filter opened 60% more at the strike): a timbre, the level untouched. Meanwhile the other voices' notes take -2 dB of shelf and at most -0.5 dB of level. **Register compensation**: an entry in the top voice gets 0.35 of these cues (+1 dB), an inner voice's or the bass's the whole (the ear already follows the top). **Switches, off by default**: VIBRATO (6-12 cents at 5.3 Hz after 0.15 s on the subject's notes; a plucked note, harpsichord or lute, only a 3-cent shimmer) and STEREO (the entering voice drawn to 35% of its place, near the centre). **The effect**, one per testbench fugue and per subject, as soft layer notes through `chip.note` (small one-oscillator patches, released with the note, D210) or the voice's own instrument echoed or doubled, and a send to the room: I/1 *4' FLUTE* (a sine an octave above on every note, more room: serene, stretto-dense, so a sustained colour without new accents); I/2 *GLASS SPARK* (a short FM-bell ping an octave up on the head and the eighths only, the skeleton of the sixteenths: sharp, never on every note); I/4 subject 1 *16' PEDAL* (a sine an octave below, a larger room: the solemn cross-motif grounded), subject 2 *SECOND CHOIR* (the voice doubled 7 cents off, a harpsichord's second 8': the running quavers thickened without new attacks), subject 3 *BELL* (a small bell two octaves up on each note: the repeated notes ring); I/5 *TRUMPET BITE* (an FM-brass blip through an opening filter at each attack: the overture's dotted runs festive); I/8 *NAZARD* as written (a faint twelfth above, an organ mutation: learned), *MIRROR 16'* inverted (the layer mirrored below), *SLOW GIANT* augmented (an octave above and below, the largest room); I/10 *TOCCATA ECHO* (a 110 ms slapback on the other side, darker, on the head and the longer notes: the two-voice texture widened, the runs not blurred); I/11 *LEAP SPARK* (a short triangle pluck an octave up on the head and on each note reached by a leap of a fourth or more: the dance's springs); Contrapunctus XIV subject 1 *4' PRINCIPAL* (a faint organ stop an octave above, large room: stile antico), subject 2 *CHORUS* (a detuned double -9 cents, a slapback on its longer notes: the running subject lively), subject 3 *B-A-C-H BELLS* (each note of the name rung two octaves up over a soft 16', the largest room). **The other 41 fugues**: *ROOM* (a send to the room and the faintest 4' on the longer notes). Layer levels 0.03-0.07 against the instruments' 0.4 (about -16 to -22 dB); echoes and doubles at 0.14-0.28 of the note. **The player's voice is treated the same** (D208: the voices are equals): its subject notes (and the notes riding after them) emphasised, its effect's layers stopped when the thumb lets go, its other notes dulled during another voice's entry. **Cost**: a shelf where asked (one filter; the sampled path's touch shelf and the subject's share one), a send (one gain), a vibrato (two nodes); a layer note 6-7 nodes. Measured on the fake context over 90 s: x1.18 to x1.5 the nodes made (I/10's echo the most), every node released, at most 210 wired at once. **LAB** in the bench's ⚙: SUBJECT SOUND, EFFECT, VIBRATO, STEREO (`tb-lab-v1` in localStorage), read as a game begins. Not yet judged by ear. | `src/touch/subject-fx.ts` (`SubjectSound`, `SubjectCues`, `TAILORED`, `GENERIC`, `playFx`, `setLab`); `src/touch/chip.ts` (`Play.tone`, `crisp`, `vib`, `room`, `detune`; `shelf`, `vibrato`, `send`); `src/bench/proto-game.ts` (`subj`); `src/bench/LabSettings.tsx`; `src/bench/BenchApp.tsx`; `test/subject-fx.test.ts` |
| D214 | Experiment E2, owner: "one is not following a single voice, but the subjects in all of its entries (further complexity if the fugue is double or triple!) … it might make sense to use the highway format for the challenging subject play". **SUBJECT**, a new phone mode (`subject`, upright, the whole screen), on HIGHWAY's road and machinery (five lanes, two rows of pads, slides, EASY's levels, the crowd, the multiplier, flames, sustain, the result screen, captures and bosses). **The line**: every note of every entry in `f.entries` (every theme of a double or triple fugue, subject and answer, inverted and augmented; not the countersubjects), in whichever voice it lies, from the starting point to the end; between entries the player rests while the whole fugue plays. Those notes are taken out of the accompaniment (by voice, onset and pitch) and the player sounds them, each in its own voice's voicing (equal voices, D208: the sound of a note is its voice's, not one fixed voice's; also its ornament, its riders, a muffled miss, the swoosh). **Stretto rule: the newest entry wins.** When an entry begins while another still sounds, the player leaves the older one at the new entry's first note (the new statement is the event) and the older one's remaining notes go back to the accompaniment; two entries beginning together: the first subject's (theme 1 before 2 before 3), then the upper voice's, the other accompaniment throughout. Only entries beginning at or after the starting point are played. An entry's chord (I/2's soprano at bar 29) is struck as one. **Lanes**: the contour within each entry planned by `planLanes` on five lanes; every entry's first note is free (any lane; an open bar in the entry's colour) and never rides. **Whose subject**: the gems in their voice's colour (`inkOf`; the pads keep the lanes' colours); the entry under way named at the top (SUBJECT · ALTO, SUBJECT 2 · TENOR, · INVERTED, · AUGMENTED; larger as it begins); the next entry's name riding beside its first note down the road; during a rest of up to four bars, a diamond at the road's far end pulsing on the beat and IN n counting it in. The other voices stay faint behind the lanes, by pitch (D208), the older entries' remainders among them. **Scored** over the subject notes only; entries captured as usual (a stretto-cut entry over the notes played); the grade not recorded against any voice on the wheel; the seals judged with every voice played (an entry wholly lost to a simultaneous one cannot be captured, so a STRETTO seal there stays out of reach). Accuracy against the recording's detected onsets: bar-line map median 18–46 ms (95th 52–119); DTW 12–33 ms; between anchors (leave-one-out) median 7–12 ms, 95th 17–28 ms; reliable onsets 37% (I/5, the French overture's dotted rhythms) to 87% (I/10). **Clock** (`her-clock.ts`): a monotone piecewise-linear tick ↔ seconds map in place of the steady tempo and the final ritard (hers is in it), from the start slider's tick; the count-in at her tempo over the first bar played; the windows in ticks at her mean tempo over the passage (so they follow her, ±her rubato). **Playback** (`with-her.ts`): her track fetched (beside the page, else the Internet Archive) and decoded once (one fugue at a time; released on quitting a game, kept for PLAY AGAIN), an AudioBufferSourceNode on the chip's AudioContext through a gain into the chip's limiter (past the room and the compressor), `start(when, offset)` at the passage's first tick (decoded late: a moment on, where the game will be); on the audio clock as the game's notes are, so `chip.lag` applies to both alike. **Fade**: 0 below a run of 8, a smoothstep to 0.85 at 32, scaled by the recent accuracy (an average, each hit 8% towards 1, each slip 15% towards 0; full at 90%, none at 60%); up with setTargetAtTime (τ 0.7 s, about 2 s), down τ 0.25 s; a slip (miss, wrong, stray: `combo = 0`) τ 40 ms (out within 150 ms) and none for two bars; the others' voices (a new `chip.others` bus; the player's lead notes go straight to the master) down to 35% at her peak; a hidden tab or the phone turned hushes her (two bars); the end and a quit fade her out over half a second. **Tuning**: equal temperament at her A while she plays, the player's tuning restored after (`setTuning` now takes any A from 400 to 470). Title card: WITH KIMIKO ISHIZAKA · TEMPO HERS (· LOADING…, or HER RECORDING CANNOT BE REACHED, the game then on its own); the result screen credits her (The Open Well-Tempered Clavier, 2015, CC0). Checked in Chromium on I/1 from the start and from bar 14: the recording's actual output (an analyser on her source, matched against the decoded track) at the place her map gives for the game's tick, to the sample, at 4 to 30 s; the browser's decoding identical in timing to librosa's (envelope lag 0). | D217 | Owner on SUBJECT (D214): "when entries overlap… we might need something more creative for those moments. Subject mode would be precisely capable of accommodating multiple simultaneous subjects somehow, even if it requires double pressings, and perhaps it would just require special moves (not a perfect reproduction of the two voices). Also, there are way too many crossbars … it sucks when it occurs so much and so often"; "we now have two lines of buttons. Maybe we can explore that somehow?" **Stretto in two rows.** The entries sounding at a moment (begun, their last note not yet over; a voice holds one entry at a time, its newest) are ranked newest first (a tie: the first subject, then the upper voice). The newest is the main line; the next newest, while it sounds, is the second line; any older entry goes back to the accompaniment (I/4's strettos of three and four entries: the two newest are played); when the newer entry ends first, the older one becomes the main line again (I/8's augmentations). The two rows of pads become two hands: the upper row plays the newer entry, the lower row the older one; outside a stretto either row plays the single entry, as before. A note is right only in its row (and lane, per EASY's level; each row judged from its own last lane); a tap in the other row on that row's note is wrong (✕ OTHER ROW). The older entry is reduced to its special moves: only its notes on a beat or of half a beat or more are struck; the quicker ones ride (D191: played for the player, after the struck note before them); its long notes are holds as ever; each stretto's first lower-row note is struck and free. Double presses (one per row) are expected. **Drawn**: the main line's gems solid in their voice's colour, the second line's hollow (an outline over the dark road, a notch at the foot; its free note a hollow bar, its riders hollow dots); a beat before a stretto STRETTO! and, on the pads, STRETTO · TWO ROWS, the upper row framed in the newer entry's colour (NEW: ALTO), the lower dashed in the older's (OLDER: SOPRANO), both while it lasts; at the top the newer entry's name over "+ SUBJECT · SOPRANO · LOWER ROW". **Scored**: a stretto (each stretch where the same two entries are played together) cleared without a slip scores twice an entry's bonus, STRETTO ×2!; each of its entries is captured as usual (over both lines). The testbench's strettos: I/1 12, I/4 42, I/8 20, Contrapunctus XIV 12, I/2 none. **Fewer crossbars** (all highways: phone HIGHWAY, SUBJECT, computer HIGHWAY and its lessons): a note is free (an open bar, any lane or key) only at the first note, at a new contour's start (SUBJECT: an entry's first note, a stretto's first lower-row note), and after a real rest: a beat or more from the end of whatever sounded last (half a bar where a beat passes in under half a second). D187's rule (free after any long note, or after a rest of a quarter beat) is withdrawn. Where it used to free a note (after a long note or a short rest) the lane planner may now recentre silently: the move still goes the line's way (further right for a rise, further left for a fall; a leap two lanes, so + LEAPS still holds) but as far as suits what follows, at no cost, with no open bar drawn. Measured over the eight testbench fugues (every voice from the start for HIGHWAY; SUBJECT from the start, both rows): HIGHWAY 2,662 open bars of 8,922 struck notes (29.8%, 17.7 a minute of music) → 206 (2.3%, 1.4 a minute); SUBJECT 653 of 1,945 (33.6%, 16.7 a minute) → 315 of 2,428 (13.0%, 8.1 a minute; nearly all of them entry heads, which stay free); the edge piles (a judged move drawn against the line at a wall) 4.7% → 5.3% on five lanes, 0.15% → 0.20% on nine. **Tests**: the two lines exactly the ranking's (none also in the accompaniment, every note of the fugue sounding once); every entry's head free and struck in either line; a perfect two-row run through all eight testbench fugues (I/1, I/2, I/4, XIV among them) all hits in TIMING and EXACT LANES, every entry captured, every stretto cleared, every rider sounded; never more than two entries struck at once; the second line's quick notes ride; the rows judged (before a stretto the lower row plays the main line; in it a lower-row note struck in the upper row is wrong and loses the stretto); crossbars under 4% on HIGHWAY, free only after a rest of a beat. | `subject-line.ts` (`subjectLine`: `second`, `secondHeads`, `strettos`), `proto-game.ts` (`lineTracks`, `Track.fresh`, `PAD_SPLIT`, `rowTrack`, `inStretto`, `strettoNow`, `nearestPending`, `laneBy`, `lanesOf`, `isFree`, `restBefore`, `soft`, `judgeStrettos`, `strettoState`, entries over both lines), `protos.ts` (`planLanes`'s `soft`; SUBJECT's and the HIGHWAYs' how), `paint-proto.ts` (`secondLine`, `strettoRows`, the stretto names, ✕ OTHER ROW, flames by line), `lessons.ts` (the free rule), `BenchApp.tsx` (the preview and the seal over both lines); `test/bench-subject.test.ts`, `test/bench-protos.test.ts` |
| D218 | Owner: "more points to perfect legato … not only in SUBJECT mode but in general: a perfect legato is more risky than a boring staccato … the same for holding notes for the right amount of time and releasing them to respect pauses: rewarded and noticed, and penalised if not complied." **Articulation scored** in every testbench mode where a note lasts while held (HIGHWAY, SUBJECT, LADDER, TWO VOICES; the computer's keys by keyDown/keyUp; TIMING too). **LEGATO**: a note joined in the score to the one before (no rest of a quarter beat or more, counted from the note just before it, riders included) struck by a new touch or key while that note still sounds (another thumb or key holding it, or let go no more than 60 ms and a tenth of its length before) scores its points again ×0.5, ×1, ×1.5, at most ×2 along an unbroken chain (so ×1.5 … ×3 in all); a detached strike on a joined note is no slip and scores as it is, but breaks the chain, as do a slip and a rest held through. A **slide** is not legato (owner: "slides are less risky than a proper legato with multiple orchestrated touches. Slide is cheap"): it sounds joined and scores as a plain note, earns no LEGATO bonus and breaks the chain. **HELD**: a note of 250 ms or more, let go (or given way to the next note) within 75%–110% of its length or ±80 ms, whichever is wider, of its written end scores 2 points a beat (1 to 8) × the multiplier; let go before half its length, **SHORT**: the crowd −0.01, no combo break; the sustain points stay. **THE REST**: a note followed by a written rest of a quarter beat or more, still held past its end + max(80 ms, a quarter of the rest): **REST!** at once, the crowd −0.03, the chain broken (no slip); let go in time: **CLEAN**, 1 × the multiplier. A note followed by riders (D191) is not judged at its end (the riders take the sound over); a chord is judged once, by its struck note. **Noticed**: the word under the verdict in the pixel font (LEGATO ×n violet, HELD / CLEAN green, SHORT / REST! red), LEGATO ×n under the score while a chain runs; on the road a faint violet slur over every coming joined pair and a bright one across the strike line as a legato is played (not a slide); in the box a pixel slur between the two blocks with the word, and the bonus points rising; the result screen: LEGATO % (CHAIN n) · HELD % · RESTS %. | `src/bench/articulation.ts` (`legatoBonus`, `holdBonus`, `restAfter`, `releaseVerdict`, `artLine`); proto-game.ts (`joinedAt`, `judgeEnd`, `overstays`, `art`, `chain`, `flash`, `ties`; `Held.open`); box-fx.ts `Pop.from`/`ink`; paint-proto.ts `articulation`, `slurs`; BenchApp's result line; test/bench-articulation.test.ts; bench-protos' LEGATO playthrough lets go at a rest. |

The judging is already right once the delay is tapped (D173); what is wrong is the player's own note, heard one delay after the touch. **Noticing other headphones**: while the app is open the reported output latency is read every 2 s and the browser's `devicechange` heard; a jump over 50 ms or a device change raises a small banner, HEADPHONES CHANGED? · RECALIBRATE (5 s), which opens the tapping (not during a game). The delay tapped is kept per listening setup (the output's name where the browser gives it, else the reported latency in 50 ms buckets, a neighbouring bucket accepted; else one setup) and given back when that setup returns. **The calibration, quicker** (the bench's and the phone game's): twelve clicks 0.6 s apart, the first three to settle in, it ends at eight taps (a tap from 0.1 s early to 0.5 s late); the median, taps further from it than 40 ms or three median deviations left out, refused under five taps or if the rest spread over 80 ms; shown in ms with a verdict, WIRED-LIKE (80 ms or less) or BLUETOOTH. **The screen's delay, apart**: SET BY WATCHING, a gem falling onto a line every 0.6 s, tapped as it crosses (seen, no click), the same arithmetic; the box and the road are drawn that far ahead of the clock as heard (`viewNow`), the judging untouched. **BLUETOOTH MODE** (⚙ HEADPHONES: AUTO, ON, OFF; AUTO on when the delay tapped, else the one reported, is over 80 ms): each of the player's notes coming is sounded ahead on the band's schedule at its written time (so it is heard on time), its chord, ornament and riding notes (D191) with it, through its own gate at 0.3 of its level (armed); a right touch in the window confirms it (the gate raised, the note then held while the touch lasts and let go with it; no second note struck); no touch by 90 ms (as heard) past its written time: faded fast (about 40 ms), so a miss is heard briefly (the delay plus the wait); a wrong touch never confirms it: it fades and the wrong note sounds, muffled, as before; a touch after the fade plays its note as without the mode. The window's late side 20 ms wider (the headphones' jitter). Releases, legato, HELD and REST (D218) are judged from the touches as before; a slide onto an armed note takes it (on time) instead of gliding late. Not while the music waits for each note (the lessons). Text in the panel: "Bluetooth adds 120–300 ms. Calibrate after connecting. Wired or the phone's speaker is best." | `src/touch/headphones.ts` (`OutputWatch`, `setupKey`, `lagFor`, `withLag`, `btOn`, `quickLag`, `verdictOf`), `src/touch/chip.ts` (`Gate`, `gate()`, `Play.into`, `visual`), `src/touch/lag.ts` (`offsetOf`'s period and early side), `src/touch/Panels.tsx` (`Tapper` quick, `VisualTapper`), `src/bench/proto-game.ts` (`arm`, `watchArmed`, `fadeArmed`, `ARM_LEVEL`, `ARM_WAIT_MS`, `BT_LATE_MS`, `viewNow`, `view`), `src/bench/protos.ts` (`Options.bt`), `src/bench/Headphones.tsx` (`useHeadphones`, `HeadphonesFold`), `src/bench/BenchApp.tsx` (the banner, the calibration panel); test/bench-bluetooth.test.ts |

Our engraver lays out a whole fugue in 1-6 ms (node; 20-30 ms first time in Chromium) and paints a 512 px tile in 3-9 ms. The OWTC MusicXML was not needed (the strip is drawn from pieces.json, the same data the game plays, so the score and the clock cannot disagree; its rights question, data/sources/bach-wtc/SOURCE.md, is not new). **strip.ts** (pure, tested): the piece as one line of music: notes split at bar lines and into written values (tied: a half tied to an eighth), rests in the gaps (off-beat rests to the beat first, a bar's silence a whole-bar rest), chords within a voice, accidentals by bar and staff against the signature (not repeated across a tie), beams by the beat (a compound meter's dotted beat, 3/8 by the bar, else a quarter at most), stems by voice on a shared staff, by place on a single one; an engraver's spacing (every onset a column, 1.65 sp for a sixteenth plus 1.05 sp per doubling, room before bar lines, accidentals and after dots); `xOf(tick)` piecewise linear through the columns, monotonic. **scroll-score.ts**: the strip painted in 512 CSS px tiles (resolution at most 2x) as they come into view, one ahead, at most six kept (about 19 MB on a 390 px box), reused on eviction; each frame blits two or three tiles so that `xOf(now)` stands under the band (the scroll speeds up where the music is dense, as the videos do), then draws only what changes: a blue halo on the player's hit notes, red heads on misses, the band, the title, the keys (widths cached), the fixed plate of clefs and key signature (time signature at the strip's start). GERUBACH: the player's voice blue, the subject's entries black with a bracket and a red circled number (1, 2, 3… in time), the other notes grey; bar numbers. SCORE: the player's staff full size and tinted, the others at 0.7, voice names, the system's bracket, bar numbers, entries bracketed S / A, the bar sounding tinted, a blue cursor. **The side bars keep the game's own ladder** (`Template.rows: false`; `uiRows` returns null): the bars map the player's range as without an interface, the score only scrolls; the box (LADDER on phones, every computer mode that shows a box) is where it draws. Measured (headless Chromium, 844x390 phone landscape at 2x, LADDER I/2 alto, autoplay): 60 fps. Glyphs added (rests, 32nd flags, time signatures; tools/ui/extract-glyphs.mjs). | D230 | Owner's idea, refined (an experiment, **off by default**): **SCALE KEYS**, a second keyboard layout for the computer's HIGHWAY, chosen as KEYS: HOME ROW · SCALE (EXPERIMENT) on the HIGHWAY title card and in ⚙ (stored with the pick, `keys`; not in the lessons). **The home row is the home key's scale**: A S D F G H J K = degrees 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8, do to do, the tonic the piece's key (`f.key`); a minor key's scale the natural minor with the raised 7th (the leading tone stays home). **Altered notes like a piano's black keys**, judged by the note's spelling against the home scale's degree (its staff step from the kern encoding, which pieces.json carries for every note, so no spelling had to be guessed; Contrapunctus XIV's, from MIDI, as D minor spells): raised the row above (1 W, 2 E, 4 T, 5 Y, 6 U; 3 R, 7 I rare), lowered the row below (2 X, 3 C, 5 B, 6 N, 7 M; 4 V, 1 Z rare); a double alteration the same row. In C minor E♭ and A♭ are home, E♮ R, A♮ U, F♯ T, B♭ M, D♭ X. So the upper row lights up as the music goes to the dominant side, the lower to the subdominant side. **The tonic folds**: A or K, the line's way from the key before (rising into it K, falling A; already at that edge, the same; the first note A if the line then rises, K if it falls); a repeated pitch keeps its key, and SPACE still answers it. **Judging**: the note's own key (a chord: any of its keys; the tonic at home either A or K); timing as usual; EASY's levels ignored except TIMING (any key on time); no open bars; the rows are degrees, so no row dynamics (one level) and no trill row; a wrong key sounds its own pitch. **Drawing** (paint-scale.ts, apart from paint-desk.ts): eight home lanes coloured by degree (the tonic gold at both ends); an altered note a narrower, taller dark gem on the seam between two lanes, raised a little with an amber rim (above) or lowered with a blue rim (below); every gem labelled with its key's letter; under the road the three rows as a key-strip (lit while down; R I V Z dim), HOME: C MINOR, and an AWAY meter (of the last 24 notes passed, the share above and below); G's gold lane and the hands' tints not drawn (the lanes are degrees, not fingers). A diagram in ModeDiagram (the three rows as a piano) and three rules lines (`SCALE_HOW`) when chosen. **How far each Book I fugue leaves home** (every voice, by spelling): the major fugues 90-96% on the home row (I/5 96.0, I/1 91.6), the minor 72-84% (I/10 E minor 71.6% home, 11.5% above, 16.9% below; I/12 74.8/14.5/10.7; I/2 84.1/8.5/7.4); mean 86.2% home, 7.4% above, 6.4% below. A minor fugue's raised 6th (melodic minor) shows above and its lowered 7th below, so its two rows are busier than a major one's even at home. **Checked**: a perfect run on I/1 and I/2, every voice, all hits (SPACE on repeats); in Chromium at 1280×760 (I/2, alto, driven by the keys). Fix: a grand staff shares one inner gap (`grandGap`: the larger inner reach + 2 spaces, 6 to 9 spaces); staff lines across every tile, the plate and the strip from one `staffLineYs`; the bracket red (the player's in blue) and kept 1.5 spaces off the neighbouring staff; SCORE picks the clef with the fewest ledger lines (`clefOf`), a tenor also the treble an octave down (`g8`, an 8 under the clef), an alto or soprano never; the plate places clefs and key signatures by the staff's lines. Voices stay: S(+A) treble, (T+)B bass, stems by voice. | `src/touch/ui/scroll-score.ts`, `src/touch/ui/strip.ts`; test `test/ui-scroll-score.test.ts` (staff and y of every I/12 head, alto within 4 ledger lines, gap 6–9 spaces, plate and strip lines at the same y through the past, SCORE clefs) |

| D268 | THE SCORE SHEET. The engine keeps each point's source (notes, legato, held notes, subjects, cadences, bonuses) and the right notes' timing (PERFECT/GOOD). The result weighs four qualities (accuracy 40, timing 20, articulation 20, subjects captured 20; one with nothing to judge left out) into PERFORMANCE /100; a finished run's FINAL SCORE is its points plus points × performance/200; the best final kept per piece (NEW BEST). A failed run shows the bar reached of the last, the share of the piece, and what cost it most (misses, wrong notes or stray taps). In play, LADDER's right bar shows live ACCURACY, SUBJECTS captured/come and the CROWD meter. Grade and stars unchanged. | WTC 1.2 |

| D272 | THE READINGS: how three books say each Book I fugue should be played, from their words and music examples only (the owner: textual descriptions, no listening to recordings): Keller (1965; Eng. 1976), Bodky (1960), Riemann (1890; Eng. 1893). Extracted with quotations, pages and caveats (docs/wtc/readings/), turned into data/touch/readings.json: the subject note by note (joined to the next, detached, short, accented), its level and shape, a dynamic plan by bar. Two or three a fugue, the first (Keller, then Bodky, then Riemann) its default; the others open once the fugue is finished. ♪ HEAR THE SUBJECT on each card: the subject drawn with the reading's marks and played so, the author's words below. In play the marks are drawn over the player's subject notes; each ask met (the join or the gap into a note, an accent, staccato, the subject's level; elsewhere the plan's level) scores THE READING, a quality on the score sheet; legato earns nothing where the reading asks detached. | WTC 1.4 |
| D273 | THE TUTORIAL plays with the other voice: after the right hand's opening alone (waiting), the right hand plays itself (drawn in the box) and the player enters with the left hand's answer on the beat; thumbs, legato, loud and soft and the slide in the left hand against it; the subject in the right hand against the left. The thumbs coach asks for turns, either thumb first (the owner: "the key idea is alternation, not a specific order"). | WTC 1.4 |
| D274 | Fugues keep the recordings' tempo whatever the reading (the owner: "fugues should not be too fast"; Bodky's marks reached ♩ = 160 in No. 4); a book's metronome mark is only shown. | WTC 1.4 |

| D277 | A fugue's subject keeps its ornaments in every entry (the owner: "trills on subjects ... absent"): the encoding carries a subject's trill only where it is printed, and the books ask for it throughout (Bodky I/14 "always", I/23 "goes on every entry"; Keller I/23, I/24). An ornament on a note of the first entry, or on the same note of two entries, or named by the books where the encoding has none (I/12 the trill on G, I/14 on G sharp, II/12 the mordent on F), is carried to that note of every entry, upright, inverted or transposed (not augmented, diminished or later subjects), when it is a quaver or longer. E.g. I/6's trill now in 16 entries of 16 (was 11), II/1's in 8 (was 3). The other voices' ornaments were already sounding. | WTC 1.8 |
| D278 | On a phone the player plays the ornament (the owner: "they ought to require something different from the player"): the note touched, then the thumb shaken on it: first move up for a trill, short trill or turn, down for a mordent; each move past three fifths of a row (more than a rolling thumb's wander), the other way from the last, sounds the ornament's next note (trill: upper and main in turn, four moves; mordent: lower then main, two; short trill: two; turn: upper, main, lower, main). Done before the note's end: TRILL ✓ (MORDENT, TURN) and points; not done: the plain note, nothing lost. While it is to be shaken, the thumb does not slide. The bars show the sign and SHAKE as such a note comes. The tutorial teaches it (THE ORNAMENT: Invention 1's two mordents, bars 1–2). The computer's keys keep the automatic ornament. | WTC 1.8 |

One plan of the thumbs (fingering.ts): each note a touch, the thumbs taking turns, a joined note the other thumb landing before the last lifts, a detached one lifted at three fifths, a short one at a third, loud or soft across the bar, an ornament shaken, a rising step slid where sliding is taught. Drawn: in HEAR THE SUBJECT a bar on each side of the subject (pp by the box, ff at the edge) with the thumbs hovering in, landing, holding, wobbling on an ornament, lifting, as the subject plays; in the tutorial, each step (but the whole invention) first played by the game with its thumbs on the bars (the player's touches wait), then "Now you" and a guide: the thumb the next note wants hovering where it lands (the other from the last; before the first note, either), THEN SHAKE for an ornament, SLIDE for a slid step, LIFT over a thumb whose note is over. | D281 | Ornaments on the right notes (the owner, II/13: "the trills are absent"): they were scheduled, but their neighbour came from the kern sign's case (T, W, M a whole step), the encodings' plain default, wrong for 120 of the fugues' 248 ornaments (II/13: a trill on B sharp with D natural, on A sharp with C natural; I/24: the penultimate G sharp trilled with A sharp). Now the next letter up (down) with the accidental in force: as the bar shows it (the last note of that letter in any voice since the bar began), else as a lower-case sign says (t, w, m: a half step, written deliberately: II/12's mordents with the leading note), else the key signature; never more than a whole step. | D282 | THE TUTORIAL in baby steps, after an audit (the owner: "all the basic skills needed to enjoy the game to the fullest, in baby steps from the beginning"). Each shows its goal on the screen while played and in the list of steps; a step not passed says, in numbers, what fell short ("You took turns on 4 of 10 quick notes; aim for 7"). | D285 | Slower tempi (the owner: "We should avoid excessively fast tempos in this game even if they're recommended by the experts"; II/9 and II/13 too fast). The tempo played was the median of 57 to 76 recordings (D199, D274): half of all pianists and harpsichordists went slower. Now: the recordings' 15th percentile (85% go faster), lowered further so the busiest voice keeps to 4.5 notes a second, never under the slowest recording, so every tempo is one a musician chose. Every fugue 9% to 34% slower; II/9 ♩ 107 → 86, II/13 ♩ 132 → 115; the busy toccata fugues most (I/3 93 → 68, I/10 98 → 68, I/19 105 → 69). The median is kept (`standardTempo`): difficulty, stars, tiers and the lessons' search are measured at it, so the ranking and what a player has unlocked do not move. |
| D286 | On a phone, relative moves decide, not where the thumbs rest (the owner: "a player might do all the correct alternations but still get an error due to not tapping in the exact location … Relative moves are way more important than absolute location. Some mobile phones are small!"). Measured with simulated players on five pieces: one whose every move was right but whose two thumbs rested 9 mm apart lost 22 to 41% of the notes, 14 mm apart 47 to 58%; yet one moving the WRONG way kept 26 to 96%. The cause: on a change of thumb the touch was compared with the other thumb's height, on the other bar (D208), strict about level and loose about direction. Now a change of thumb is judged from that thumb's own last touch (if within 3 s and it has not travelled since, by a slide or a line): the way from the note it played to this one, a hair's leeway for a step, a clear move for a third or more; a thumb's first touch keeps the old rule. The wide area round the drawn note (5 rows, at least 90 px) stays. Test (bench-relative): right moves with the thumbs 60 or 90 px apart, gestures at 40% of the true size, a wobbling hand: 98 to 100% right; the contour inverted, random taps, one spot: at most 20%. |

The tutorial no longer says "gold marks the subject" (D287). |
| D289 | The tutorial repaired, phase 1 of plan D (the owner's choices on the Decision Board, 26 October 2026, after the Tutorial Audit). Legato: a joined note counts if the other thumb is still down or the last one lifted no more than max(90 ms, 35% of its length) before (was min(60 ms, 10%): 31 ms for a semiquaver, so a real player passed the step 0–39%); the owner: "Legato is about smoothness of movement, not simultaneity", and every line that taught "land the next before you lift the last" now teaches a handover with no gap. A touch the system cancels counts as a lift for the join. The slide fires when the thumb reaches the next note's drawn height (at least 10 px; was a fixed 27 px against notes drawn 8 px apart: the step passed 0%), the demonstration likewise. The ornament shake: either way first, a smaller rock (0.4 of a row). Each thumb's wrong-way leeway for a step: a row (was a quarter): a step's direction is no longer judged at the present note size (an inverted contour keeps up to about 3/4 of the notes; leaps still need a clear move the right way); larger notes belong to the music box redesign. Feedback: never "0 of 0" (a skill with nothing to count reports the notes); subjects counted in the passage only (was the whole invention: "0 of 20"). Passages end on the note they lead to. After a step not passed: TRY AGAIN, WATCH AGAIN, and after three tries SKIP FOR NOW (set aside, open, marked ↷). Herr Bach in the band below the music box, his line at once, TAP TO GO ON on a phone. Next: phase 2, seven lessons of learn card, waiting practice and try. |

The report now says what took a STRAY's note (a touch or a slide, and when), counts the touches the system cancelled, and gives the play area's real width (it read "1 px"). The real MISS count (43) far exceeds the simulated (7): untouched notes or touches not registered; the new report lines will tell. |
| D291 | The owner, playing II/23's bass on an iPhone: "the bass is not loud enough… the higher voices are dominating". The classic chip's bass is a triangle wave, almost all fundamental (overtones fall as 1/n²); a phone's speaker gives almost nothing below about 500 Hz, where a fugue's bass lies. Modelled (a speaker rolling off below 600 Hz, A-weighted), the triangle bass sits 24 dB under the soprano on a phone (C3), 10 dB on a full-range speaker. On a phone (a touch screen) the bass is now a pulse, whose overtones carry the pitch into the speaker's range: 50% duty where three voices or fewer (no other voice has it), 25% in four (the tenor has 50%), at 1.3× level; it comes within about 7 dB of the soprano. Computers keep the triangle. Also from the owner's II/22 (alto, 399/447) and II/23 (bass, 300/327) reports on 1.14: the same pattern as D290 (no early touch among 53; 26 of 29 and 13 of 16 missed notes a step from the last; missed notes about three times what a simulated player misses), which 1.15–1.16 already address; nothing new to change. | chip.ts voicing, PHONE_BASS_GAIN; test/phone-bass.test.ts | WTC 1.17 |

CLASSIC CHIP, measured first (II/22 from 54 s rendered offline through chip.ts): its 12.5% pulse soprano put 16.6% of its energy above 3 kHz and 3.7% above 5 kHz (the harpsichord's soprano 12.8% and 2.1%, but dying away), held flat at 0.72 of its peak; its pulses now rise in 6 ms to a peak 6% higher, fall to 0.6 of it (time constant 80 ms) and, held past 0.3 s, sink toward 0.42 (1.2 s), as a console's volume envelope; a lowpass per pulse note open at 9 kHz for the attack closes (90 ms) to 4.5 times the note within 2.5-4.5 kHz. Same waves, same voices, triangle bass unchanged: soprano 11.3% above 3 kHz, 1.0% above 5 kHz (centroid 1832 → 1642 Hz), level unchanged (-21.2 LUFS against -21.1). GAME BOY (after the DMG: two pulse channels of 12.5/25/50/75% duty with a hardware volume envelope stepping 1/15 every n/64 s, the wave channel's 32 four-bit samples with no envelope, output unfiltered but for a DC-blocking high-pass, as Pokémon Red/Blue and Link's Awakening voice their lines: pulse leads with decaying envelopes and late vibrato, the wave channel's bass): top down a 25% pulse lead (15 stepping to 9 every 3/64 s, vibrato 12 cents at 6 Hz after 0.32 s), a 50% pulse (14 to 8 every 2/64 s), the wave channel's hollow sample (a 4-bit square, stepped shoulders), a 12.5% pulse, the wave channel's 4-bit sawtooth bass (holds); the stepping stops at a floor so long notes hold (a fugue's held notes cannot fade out as a tune's may); no lowpass, 2 ms attacks (forward, not mellow); a fugue's fourth and fifth lines reuse the DMG's kinds, each its own colour (three voices: the 25% pulse and the two wave samples). Not modelled: the 11-bit period's pitch rounding (it would detune the temperaments), the noise channel (no drums). ANALOG SYNTH (WENDY CARLOS's D209 patches remade, after Switched-On Bach's one patch per line): each patch through a 24 dB lowpass (two filters, the resonance on the first) with its own envelope; top down a brass lead (sawtooth and narrow pulse, the filter opening at each attack, vibrato 6 cents 5.4 Hz late), a hollow square clarinet, a pluck (resonant filter snapping shut; three or five voices), a nasal 12.5% pulse reed (vibrato 4.6 Hz), a sawtooth bass whose filter never closes below 1.4 kHz (phones); the lip scoops, pitch blips and detuned unisons dropped. Named for what it is, not after Moog or the record (trademarks, titles). HARPSICHORD and PIANO less robotic, by the music and the instrument, never the player (phrasing.ts `Style` from the sound chosen; the synthesized sounds as before): the harpsichord's accent is of time and length, not loudness (it has no touch dynamics): the phrasing's level swing kept at 30% (±0.5 dB), a chord struck in the others spread from the bass up 4 ms a note, the bar's first beat 8 ms late (agogic accent), repeated notes held 0.72, notes before a leap of a fourth 0.8, long notes 0.92, a broken chord's notes held 1.35 into the next (overlegato, the finger pedal), the fixed unevenness ±2 ms (it was ±6), no note more than 20 ms off; the piano (a Tureck-like clarity of lines): both Salamander layers, layer 7 for the notes the phrasing makes lighter (level under 1: the notes between the beats and low in the phrase's arch; 54 of 97 in the passage) at 2 dB under layer 12 (recorded 5 dB under, and duller), the inner voices -1 dB against the top and the bass (pianoInnerSampled), a chord's top note 8 ms ahead (the "melody lead" measured in pianists' playing), the bar's first beat 5 ms late, no pedal (notes damped at their written end, as before). The two layers are folded to mono on a phone (BUDGET), so they hold what one layer did in stereo. Measured on the renders: chord onset spread mean 6.5 ms (harpsichord) and 8.3 ms (piano) against 4.7 ms of random unevenness before; offsets -1.9..18.2 ms and -10.0..6.6 ms. Not done: the readings' articulation for the others' entries (Book I only, and the reading is the player's choice of what to be judged by), a 16' or 4' registration (twice the notes on a phone's cap, a thicker bass where clarity matters most), random timing. Of the performers only what is widely documented is drawn on: Landowska's large Pleyel with its 16' and her free, declamatory timing; Tureck's articulated, line-by-line Bach on the piano; no quotation. Levels (II/22, 54-74 s, all four voices): -21.2 to -22.6 LUFS (CLASSIC CHIP -21.2, GAME BOY -22.2, HARPSICHORD -22.6, PIANO -22.0, ANALOG SYNTH -22.1); on a phone (touch emulated, D291 voicing) through a modelled speaker (600 Hz high-pass) each bass within 5.3 dB of its soprano (GAME BOY's 0.4 dB, ANALOG SYNTH's 1.7 dB under; the harpsichord's 2 dB over). | D296 | The owner: "gameplay is too restrictive with respect to absolute vertical axis placement of thumbs … the player is not expected to meet the narrow absolute coordinates of the note in the music box … we need to allow the whole of the buttons to have more relevance". D181's window round the drawn note (five rows, at least 90 px: under half a 390 px bar, moving with the tune) is gone; LADDER judges only each thumb's own moves: from its last touch, a repeat about level (±2.5 rows), a step or third not clearly the wrong way (a row's leeway, D289), a fourth or more half a row the right way; and the line's travel, from that thumb's latest recent touch (within 3 s) a fourth or more away in the tune, a quarter row the right way, so a run must climb or fall with the hand (a third's travel is not judged: drawn steps are 8 px apart and a landing scatters as much). A thumb that travelled to a bar's end (its outer 12%) and can go no further may stay there. A thumb's first touch, or its first after a pause or a slide, is free. The bars light evenly; the ▲▼= sign stands where that thumb last touched, showing the way from its own last note. Texts: 'anywhere along it'; no pitch names (C2…C7) on the bars, and the pp…ff marks only in the dynamics lesson. Found on the way: play.html plays every fugue by a reading, and D293 counted any reading as asking for touch dynamics, so on play.html the loudness still followed the thumb; now only the dynamics lesson does, and a reading only on the subject notes it sets a level for. Simulated (four fugues): players moving at their own scale anywhere on the bar, or with the whole hand 120 px off the drawn notes, 97–100% (the window failed the latter); realistic players aiming at the drawn notes as before (II/22 92.8%, II/23 94.5%); tapping at random 25–41%, in one spot 11–68% (was ≤ 20% with the window: the price of the freedom; the pass mark stays above). | proto-game.ts (ladder verdict, remember, trailBySide, EDGE_SHARE, TRAVEL_MIN), paint-proto.ts, lessons.ts, protos.ts, i18n; test/bench-relative.test.ts (D296 test), bench-protos.test.ts | WTC 1.21 |
| D297 | Ten new sounds after the five, and ANALOG SYNTH remade, for the owner to judge (the list fifteen for now, in this order: CLASSIC CHIP, GAME BOY, HARPSICHORD (SAMPLED), PIANO (SAMPLED), SWITCHED-ON, SUPER 16-BIT, NOCTURNE 32-BIT, MEGA FM, SID 64, TRACKER, CHAMBER ORGAN, TEMPLE 64, AGENT 64, MODULAR, TOMITA); no piece opens on them (no piece's default changed). Asked for in three rounds: the research report "Polyphony on Game Hardware" (§2's rules R1-R9, §6.1-6.3's three presets; §6.4's General MIDI set not built); then the owner, who liked the five, asked for "a few more presets … covering different synth aesthetics that fit this kind of polyphonic music"; then: "Do the Tomita as well … a couple or even three niche picks that, while not ruining the polyphony, … go in some directions which are not OPTIMAL", "I also want the N64 … Bomberman 64 … Goldeneye 64 … Zelda OCT", "one or two more MOOG-ey picks. The one Wendy Carlos you made … was lackluster". **Triage** (aesthetic: made as, or why not): SNES/SPC700 sampler: SUPER 16-BIT; PlayStation SPU, Baroque-Gothic: NOCTURNE 32-BIT; Mega Drive YM2612 FM: MEGA FM; C64 SID: SID 64 (three channels, each its own wave and motion: the home-computer side the consoles miss); Amiga/MOD tracker: TRACKER (8-bit samples, hard-panned pairs: the PC/home-computer sampler era); synthesized positive organ: CHAMBER ORGAN (Bach's own medium, a stop per line); N64: two, TEMPLE 64 (Ocarina of Time's temples: flute, harp, strings, organ, a generous reverb) and AGENT 64 (GoldenEye 007 and Bomberman 64: mallet, muted brass, soft strings, electric bass, a moodier room), as the console's sound splits that way; Switched-On Bach: SWITCHED-ON (ANALOG SYNTH remade); 1970s modular/Minimoog: MODULAR (niche: thicker); Tomita: TOMITA (niche: lush). Left out: NES/VRC6 (covered by CLASSIC CHIP and GAME BOY), OPL2/OPL3 AdLib (covered by MEGA FM, thinner), General MIDI Sound Canvas (§6.4: duplicates the recorded sets), Saturn (sample-based like NOCTURNE 32-BIT), a third N64 set (the two cover its palettes). Common rules (§2): each voice its own instrument, the outer voices the most unlike (R1), the bass from harmonics, 120 Hz high-pass on the period presets (R2), vibrato only on sustained upper voices, late, at rates at least 0.6 Hz apart (R4; SWITCHED-ON's and MODULAR's lead and reed shallower, TOMITA's whistle wider, 22 cents), effects sent from some voices only (R5), pans per voice and voice count within ±0.35 (R7, `Preset.by`), in five voices the second and fourth a shade lighter (-1.5, -0.5 dB, R9); levels fixed per voice, never from the player's touch, the subject never louder (D293); a phone's bass audible (D291). The niche picks (MODULAR, TOMITA, and TEMPLE 64's reverb) bend R4/R5 a little (unison detunes, washes on inner voices), never those three. **SUPER 16-BIT** (§6.1): oboe, harpsichord (dry; the middle of three, tenor of four), clarinet (alto of four), violin (second of five), bassoon; the bench's VSCO recordings made over once decoded (sampled.ts `PERIOD`, `bake`): mono, band-limited and resampled to 16 kHz (clarinet 14, bassoon 12), a 2-pole lowpass at about 0.36 of that rate (the Gaussian interpolation; it moves with the playback rate), 11 bits, a loop made over 1.4-3.6 s (0.25 s crossfade baked; the files are 4.5 s, unlooped); the console's envelope on the recording (`Patch.rec`: attack 8-25 ms, a fall to 0.7-0.8, then an exponential fall, 2.5-3 s); the SPC echo (`Preset.fx` "spc": 80 ms, feedback 0.35 through 2.8 kHz lowpass and 300 Hz high-pass; return 1, the voices' sends 0.10-0.22, never the harpsichord); no room. **NOCTURNE 32-BIT** (§6.2): violin, harpsichord (key-up noise 12 dB under), sustained viola (tenor of four), oboe (second of five), the pipe organ's pedal to F#3 and its manual above (`SampledInstrument.above`); 22.05 kHz, lowpass 6-8.8 kHz, 13 bits; sends 0.10-0.18 to a dark hall ("hall": 25 ms pre-delay, send high-passed at 350 Hz, RT60 1.9 s (1.2 s long on a phone), return lowpassed at 5.5 kHz). The report feared the organ pedal would vanish on a phone: measured, these pedal recordings lose 3-6 dB through the modelled speaker (the cello 11-20 dB), so the organ stayed. **MEGA FM** (§6.3): two-operator FM per note (`Patch.fm`: a sine carrier, a sine modulator whose index falls on every note): reed (1:1, 2.6 → 1.6, a second modulator at 3), harpsichord (1:3, 4.5 → 0.3, plucked), organ-flute (1:2, tenor of four), brass (second of five: the index rising first), bass (1:1, 5 → 3, a second modulator at 2: the report's 3.4 → 1.3 left it 13 dB under the reed on the modelled phone); 9-bit grain before the envelope (`Patch.bits`), 7 kHz lowpass, no reverb, no room. **SID 64**: a pulse lead whose width sweeps 0.25-0.45 at 0.7 Hz (`Patch.pwm`: a sawtooth less itself delayed, the delay swung) with the only vibrato, a sawtooth, a triangle with an FM-bell partial set (the ring modulator's metal; tenor of four: a plain triangle was 13 dB under the lead on a phone), a narrow pulse sweeping faster (second of five), a 0.35 pulse bass through a resonant lowpass opening at each note, never under 1.4 kHz; attacks 2-4 ms; no arpeggios; no reverb, no room. **TRACKER**: violin, flute (alto of four), harpsichord, clarinet (fourth of five), bassoon; resampled to 8-16 kHz and held sample to sample with no smoothing (`Bake.hold`: Paula's aliasing grit), 8 bits (asked for; the report warns of 8 bits on sustained samples: the grain is under the notes, measured spectra show it above 8 kHz at 0.1-0.6%), 0.5 s loops; the tracker's own vibrato late on the held voices; voices alternating left and right (±0.3); no reverb, no room. **CHAMBER ORGAN**: synthesized stops, a registration per voice (`SHAPES` stop*: partials summed): Principal 8' with Octave 4', Gedackt 8' (hollow, its twelfth for a phone), Krummhorn 8' (tenor of four), Spitzflöte 8' (fourth of five), the pedal's Posaune 8' (a reed, never a 16' flute); a chiff at each onset (`Patch.chiff`: band-passed noise, 15-30 ms, strongest on the flutes), no tremulant, held while held, released in 60-90 ms; a small church ("church": 20 ms pre-delay, RT60 1.4 s, sends 0.08-0.14). **TEMPLE 64** (atmospheric): flute (an ocarina's place), harp, viola (tenor of four), horn (second of five), the pipe organ's pedal and manual; VSCO and organ recordings at 22 kHz, muffled (lowpass 5-6 kHz), 10 bits (the VADPCM grain); a stone temple's reverb ("cave": 30 ms pre-delay, RT60 2.6 s, sends 0.15-0.25). **AGENT 64**: xylophone (struck), muted trumpet (alto of four), strings darkened to a synth pad (16 kHz, 3.5 kHz; slow attack), horn (fourth of five), electric bass; a shorter, moodier room ("studio": RT60 1.5 s). Both from the bench's recordings (xylophone, harp, horn, trumpet, ebass newly tuned: measured within 22 cents; the harp about 8 cents flat throughout, as recorded). **SWITCHED-ON** (ANALOG SYNTH remade, id kept so a stored choice keeps it; the D294 carlos* patches kept): after Switched-On Bach's sound (Carlos's one patch per line): a bright sawtooth-and-square lead through the 24 dB ladder, resonance 4.5, the filter envelope's quick "spit" (4x, 90 ms) and resonant close; a woody, hollow square (detached, filter blip); a resonant pluck (filter snapping shut, x9 over 110 ms, amplitude dying: harpsichord-like); a nasal 12.5% pulse reed; a snapping bass (never under 1.4 kHz); every note a little out of tune, wandering slowly per voice (`Patch.drift`: ±2-3 cents, a sine of 0.05-0.17 Hz over the clock, no nodes); portamento left out (D294: on every note of a fugue it blurs the pitch). **MODULAR** (niche): two sawtooths 5-8 cents apart in each voice (thicker, a little blurring), resonance 3-8 with deeper sweeps, a Minimoog-like bouncing bass with a sub-octave square (filter floor 1.3 kHz), drift ±3-4 cents; the shared room. **TOMITA** (niche, lush; after Isao Tomita's Snowflakes Are Dancing, 1974, and Pictures at an Exhibition, 1975): a whistling sine lead coming up 45 cents into each note, breath, wide late vibrato; a choir "ah" of three detuned sawtooths through two formants (tenor of four); a breathy filtered flute; a slow string ensemble (second of five); a plain filtered bass; a space echo (330 ms, feedback 0.32, darkening) beside a long reverb ("space": RT60 2.8 s, 30 ms pre-delay), sent from the inner voices (0.25-0.30) and barely from the top (0.12) and the bass (0.05): wide pans (±0.35). Phaser not built (an extra moving co-modulation on every voice). Engine (`D297` comments): sound.ts `Patch.rec, fm, bits, hp, send, pwm, chiff, drift`, `Preset.fx, by`, `room: 0` (the shared room unwired while such a preset plays, chip.ts `period`), `FxKind`, SHAPES stop*; chip.ts `fxInput/fxSend` (each effect built at its first note, taken down when another preset plays), `SPC_ECHO`, `REVERBS` (hall, church, cave, studio, space with its echo), `darkHall`, `stairCurve`, patchNote (fm, pwm, chiff, drift, bits, hp, send), sampledNote (rec, the patch's vibrato, `relGain`); sampled.ts `PERIOD`, `bake` (half-rate storage, `BAKE_RATE`), `WIND_LOOP`, `TRACKER_LOOP`, measured tuning of the VSCO sets, `setFor` (an organ's manual above its pedal). Phone memory: every piece's plan for all of a preset's instruments together, baked notes mono at half the decoded rate: worst 47 MB (NOCTURNE 32-BIT, a prelude spanning the harpsichord's compass), under the 96 MB budget. Measured (offline renders through chip.ts, every voice played as the game plays the others; integrated LUFS; the modelled phone speaker a 600 Hz high-pass): II/22 54-74 s, all four voices: -21.1 to -22.4 for the eleven (the four kept -21.2 to -22.6; ANALOG SYNTH -22.1 before, SWITCHED-ON -21.7); each voice alone within 2.3 dB of the others of its preset (II/22; within about 3 dB in I/4 and I/2); on a phone (touch emulated, D291) the bass against the soprano through the modelled speaker: SUPER 16-BIT -5.2 dB, NOCTURNE +0.2, MEGA FM -3.0, SID 64 -4.5, TRACKER -5.9, CHAMBER ORGAN -1.1, TEMPLE 64 +0.5, AGENT 64 -3.7, SWITCHED-ON -2.9, MODULAR -2.5, TOMITA -2.9 (I/4, five voices: within 5.4 dB); I/2's first 20 s -23.2 to -25.6 (the harpsichord -24.4, the piano -24.7). The quietest inner voices on a phone: TEMPLE 64's harp (11 dB under its flute), TOMITA's flute (7 dB), SWITCHED-ON's (6.5 dB). Measured, not heard: whether each sounds like its era, and how the echoes and reverbs sit on II/22's quavers. | sound.ts PATCHES s16*/n32*/fm*/sid*/trk*/org*/sol*/mod*/tom*/t64*/a64*, SHAPES stop*, Patch, Preset, FxKind, PRESETS, sampledNow, patchFor, placeFor; chip.ts voicing, period, fxInput, fxSend, SPC_ECHO, REVERBS, darkHall, stairCurve, patchNote, sampledNote; sampled.ts PERIOD, bake, BAKE_RATE, WIND_LOOP, TRACKER_LOOP, SAMPLED, setFor, planFor, decodedBytes; PlayPop.tsx POP_SOUNDS; i18n; test/d297-presets.test.ts, chip-presets, bench-pop-sounds, fake-audio | WTC 1.23 |

The sheet replaces only the picture between the bars: the engine, its judging (D296), count-in, ritard, results, legato, ornaments' SHAKE are the same; a piece without an engraving (preludes, the Art of Fugue, the tutorial's) shows the box. Engraving: at build time, not in the page. Verovio 6.3.0's Humdrum build is 12 MB (3.7 MB gzipped) and took 0.3 s to start and 0.4–2.5 s per fugue in node on a desktop (several times that on a phone); engraved ahead (tools/sheet/engrave.ts, the prototype's Henle-like settings, one endless system, the font a constant, SHEET_FONT = Leipzig) and read into a compact drawing (glyphs by code point, lines, beams, ties; every mark of a note under its voice; x as steps from the mark before), the 48 fugues in both layouts are 11.2 MB on disk, 1.7 MB gzipped (one fugue 11–21 KB gzipped a layout, fetched when first shown; whole-fugue SVG would have been about 60 MB, 6 MB gzipped), plus the font's 40 outlines (12 KB). The kern (data/sources/bach-wtc/kern) is laid out as an Urtext's two staves (the voices on the staves the encoding names, each staff one spine whose sub-spines are its voices, the upper first: stems up) or one staff per voice (G, G an octave down or F by the voice's median); the ossia sub-spines (I/1) left out; C and alla-breve signs; Verovio's MIDI values ignore the key signature, so each note's pitch is read from its own token. 99.9% of the game's notes are found engraved (48 fugues: 46 of 51,175 notes unmatched, ornaments' and divisi's). The page: white, black ink, every voice in its colour (the game's hues darkened for paper; the player's no bigger), staves equal in the open score. It is painted once into tiles (canvases of 512 CSS px, at most twice the CSS pixel, a few kept) in a strip as wide as the piece, moved by a CSS transform; the marks of the game in an SVG in the same strip (changed only when they change): the next note ringed in its voice's colour, a miss a small red cross under its head, each entry's bracket from its first note to its last with "Subject" or "Answer" (no voice named; dashed with a dot a beat while ahead; the word stays in sight while its bracket runs past the clefs), small bar numbers; the opening's clefs and key signatures kept in a column at the left; a thin playhead in the player's colour. The playhead follows the engraving's time map (each onset's tick at its heads' x, through the player's own heads where it has a note; linear between): engraved spacing, the engine's clock (the frame's tick: count-in, final ritard and the screen's delay in it). Bars: paper, no pitch names, the coming note's light faint, the ▲▼= sign where each thumb last touched, the score and ACCURACY small at their tops; no fugue map, no trail, no embers. Measured: the playhead on the player's heads 0 ms off for every note of I/2, I/4, II/18 and II/22, every voice, both layouts, through the final ritard (test/sheet-mode.test.ts, ProtoGame.timeAt); in the page (I/2, two staves, the whole fugue auto-played, the frames read against the engine's clock) the drawn playhead crossed the heads of the notes scheduled at their written time (the other voices) a median 2.8–3.2 ms from it, 95% within 7 ms (the frames' sampling); only heads the engraver moved aside, in the slow last bars, up to 180 ms. Frame rate, Chromium emulating an iPhone 13 sideways with the CPU slowed 4× (software rendering): 47–51 fps in the sheet modes (I/2 two staves 48.5, II/22 two staves 47.1, one staff per voice 51) (the bars' canvas at 1 device pixel to the CSS pixel in the sheet mode: at 1.5, 42; at 2, 34–39), DEFAULT 28–30 fps the same way. | D299 | The owner: "is there any room for improving the experience of playing with Bluetooth headphones? Even calculating latency with the tap, the experience is still quite unsatisfactory and not super fun. Clunky, weird ghost tempo, latency delays, not fluid at all … do some presets work better than others with respect to latency? Is there a minimal latency preset we could devise?" **What was wrong** (D223's BLUETOOTH MODE driven with a simulated player, sd 45 ms, one note in eight untouched, through a 200 ms delay, I/2's soprano, five runs, 1,320 notes): every note was heard twice over: armed at 0.3 (−10.5 dB) on time, then raised to full by the touch, which the ear gets a whole delay later (median 197 ms after the onset; 147 ms at a 150 ms delay, 297 ms at 300 ms): a second accent a delay behind every note, the "ghost tempo"; each note untouched (176) sounded soft for 300 ms before it was faded (2 × the delay + 90 ms); notes were held on until the touch's release reached the ear (median 165 ms past their written ends: legato smeared into the next); a touch after the fade struck its note a delay late (10); and the confirm made loudness depend on the touch, against D293. Without the mode (AUTO off: no delay tapped, nothing reported) each of the player's notes is heard a whole delay after the touch. The screen was drawn from `currentTime`, which moves a block at a time (in Chromium, emulated iPhone, 11 of 119 frames drawn without the music moving, the step per frame varying 27%; on a Bluetooth route the blocks can be larger). **Now** (BLUETOOTH MODE replaced; `bt` keeps its switch: AUTO, ON, OFF): the whole piece, the player's voice too, is on the audio clock's schedule (proto-game.ts `arm`): each of the player's notes at its written time and length, at the level a right touch on time gives (NEUTRAL_D: never louder or softer for how it is played), its chord (a swept chord's notes SWEEP_GAP_S apart), its ornament realised, its riders, a traced line's notes, the echo row's level, the subject's layers, a prelude's pedal bourdon from its start to its end, all ahead as the band's are; so everything is heard in time through the delay. A touch makes no sound (`touchNote`: silent; no swoosh, no chime, no ornament shake heard): it scores, judged as before against the music as heard and drawn (the clock as heard less the tapped delay; the screen drawn on it, D223's screen delay kept); holds, releases, legato, slides, shakes, pedals' share and the charge judged from the touches as before; letting go never cuts a note. **A missed note sounds all the same, at the same level** (provisional: the owner's open Decision 8, missed note silent / softer / sounds anyway; in this mode silent or softer cannot be done in time: the miss is known only a delay after the note is heard). The window 20 ms wider early and 25 ms late (the headphones' jitter; D290's halfway split and floor unchanged). The clock as heard smoothed in this mode (chip.ts `smooth`, headphones.ts `SmoothClock`: performance.now() plus an offset following `currentTime` with a 0.5 s time constant, snapped past 50 ms, never going back; the audio clock without its steps, so no drift; `getOutputTimestamp()` not used: its time already has the browser's latency estimate taken off, which the tapped delay would count twice, D191); emulated: no frame without motion, the step varying 12% (the emulator's own frame timing). The tapping calibration on the smoothed clock too. **Every run retunes the delay** (a Bluetooth route's delay settles by tens of ms over the first half hour): after a run in this mode with a tapped delay, half the median lateness of the notes struck right, at most 30 ms a run, only with 16 notes or more, their middle half within 120 ms and the median beyond ±15 ms (`driftOf`); the result says SOUND DELAY a → b MS (TUNED TO YOUR PLAYING). **⚙ on play.html**: with the mode on, what it does ("the whole piece plays on time through the delay; your touches score but make no sound"); the clearest sounds for Bluetooth, suggested, never chosen (`BT_SOUNDS`: CLASSIC CHIP, GAME BOY, HARPSICHORD (SAMPLED), SID 64, TRACKER: every voice's attack 5 ms or less, no echo or hall of their own); CHECK THE DELAY (eight clicks, a light flashing as each is heard by the delay in force: together, right; the light first, too short; the click first, too long); the last Bluetooth delay tapped kept on the device (`tb-bt-lag-v1`) and offered, USE MY LAST BLUETOOTH DELAY (n MS), never applied by itself (D260); the pop-up's line HEADPHONE DELAY n MS IS ON · BLUETOOTH MODE. The bench's ⚙ HEADPHONES the same. **Presets and latency**: no sound changes the delay (the headphones' codec and radio, 144–274 ms measured for AirPods generations; every preset goes through the same graph; a convolver or echo adds its tail, not delay, to the dry note); what differs is how sharp a note's start is to play to (attacks 1–4 ms against TOMITA's choir and AGENT 64's strings at 120 ms, long echoes and halls); in this mode no note waits for a touch, so the attack no longer adds to any touch-to-sound delay. | D300 | The owner on D298's sheet mode: (1) "it's not good that the music disappears after played … The visible sheet scrolling should make both things to come and things played visible, not only things to come"; (2) "the subject can be further highlighted by a background of some shade"; (3) "In the one staff per voice mode, we don't need color coding as much as with the redux mode … a more sober less colourful interface"; (4) "the kern engravings strike me as a bit childish and too round. Any options for doing it a bit more Henle?". (1) The playhead 35% into the score right of the clefs' column (D298: 30% of the whole box, of which the column took 15–25%: little more than a beat of what was played stood in sight before the column covered it): about a third of the music in sight is what was played, two thirds what is coming (the player reads ahead); on an iPhone sideways 39–53% from the box's left edge over the 48 fugues, both layouts, whatever the key signature. What was played stays until it leaves at the left, under a veil of white at 20% (a shade lighter, not faded); the column's fade 6 px (was 18). (2) Each entry (Subject, Answer) on a band from its first note to its last (0.9 space beyond each head, rounded corners): on the two staves hugging its voice's heads (0.9 space above the highest and below the lowest; the other voice of the staff mostly outside), in the voice's colour at 12%; in the open score over its staff (a space above and below), a warm neutral at 14%; announced (ahead) at 45% of that, with the dashed bracket; the bracket and its word kept (the band says where, the word what). (3) ONE STAFF PER VOICE in black ink, every voice: the staves tell the voices apart; the player's staff marked by a rule in the player's colour at the clefs (3 px, the staff's height), the next note's ring and the playhead in the player's colour, the brackets and words dark grey, the legend grey but for the player's voice. The soberest that still finds the staff at a glance (coloured heads on one staff only would make the player's line the one coloured thing on the page again). TWO STAVES keeps a colour a voice. (4) Why it looked childish: Leipzig's large, nearly round heads; the tiles painted at 2 device pixels and stretched to the iPhone's 3 (soft edges make heads rounder, hairlines thicker); every head coloured; heavy staff lines and stems. Nine renders of I/2 bars 9–12 (Leipzig as today, Leipzig/Bravura/Gootville/Leland with fine lines, a tuned Leland, colour and black, 2× and 3×), painted by the game's own painter at the game's size, for the owner to pick (a comparison page beside the session's work). Chosen now (a constant, tools/sheet/engrave.ts SHEET_STYLE = "henle", the others in SHEET_STYLES): Leland (MuseScore's font after traditional plates: smaller oval tilted heads, traditional flags and clefs), staff lines 0.10 space (0.13), stems 0.12 (0.18), bar lines 0.16 (0.25), ledger lines 0.16 (0.25), beams' slope at most 4 (6), spacing 0.31 / 0.62 (0.30 / 0.60; Leland's glyphs make it about 10% wider: a phone still shows more than a bar ahead); Bravura is heavier and rounder, Gootville an older, heavier hand. Verovio 6.3 has no beam thickness (refused as an engraving default). The tiles at the screen's own resolution (up to 3): a canvas is handed to the compositor again at every commit (Chromium's trace: LayerTreeHost::DoUpdateLayers half the frame at 3×), so each tile, once painted, becomes a still image (the canvas encoded off the frame, swapped when decoded). Measured (Chromium emulating an iPhone 13 sideways, CPU slowed 4×, the voice auto-played, a tap every 2.5 s, 15 s): I/2 two staves 53.7 fps, II/22 two staves 51.3, I/2 one staff per voice 53.8, II/22 51.3 (D298: 47–51); at 3× with canvases 36.8–40.5, at 2× 46–47. Re-engraved: 97 files, 11.2 MB, 1.76 MB gzipped (9.5–39 KB a fugue's layout), Leland's outlines 18 KB (glyphs-Leipzig.json gone); 46 of 51,175 notes unmatched, as before; the playhead's sync tests unchanged. Open: the open score's system bracket shows its hooks at the clefs' column; the beams' weight (Verovio's); the owner's pick among the candidates. | tools/sheet/engrave.ts (SHEET_STYLES, SHEET_STYLE, --style/--out); public/sheet/*.json, glyphs-Leland.json; sheet-format.ts (SHEET_FONT, paperInk, markInk, playheadX, entryBand); sheet-view.ts (the playhead, the veil, the bands, the open score's ink and staff mark, the tiles at 3× made still); bench.css; BenchApp.tsx (the legend); test/sheet-mode.test.ts | WTC 1.26 |
| D301 | Two faults the owner found. (1) Portuguese words lost their accents in the music box ("why are there no accents and diacritics in Portuguese words like 'você'"): its 3 by 5 pixel font had none, so D283 stripped them (VOCÊ drawn VOCE). The font now draws each letter's marks in its own pixels: acute, grave, circumflex, tilde and diaeresis in two rows above the letter, the cedilla below. (2) HEAR THE SUBJECT went on playing over the round ("not stopping playback when one presses buttons like starting the round or changing subject version"): its notes were scheduled and never kept. They are now kept and stopped when a round starts, when another reading is chosen (the new one replaces it), and when the card closes; a playback still loading its samples when superseded is dropped. Also: the Sound Test page now keeps scores and the comment on the device as well and uploads them when it connects (the owner's round-three answers never reached the page's store). | box-fx.ts (letters, MARKS), BenchApp.tsx (hearSounds, stopHearing) | WTC 1.25 |
| D302 | The owner rated D297's fifteen sounds on an iPhone (harpsichord 8, piano 8, CLASSIC CHIP 7, SID 64 7, MODULAR 7, SWITCHED-ON 6, GAME BOY 6, NOCTURNE 32-BIT 6, TOMITA 5, TRACKER 5, SUPER 16-BIT 4, MEGA FM 3, CHAMBER ORGAN 3, TEMPLE 64 3, AGENT 64 3): "Improve the ones i graded low. Usually this will be due to the sound being boring, dull, flat, uninteresting"; "I had the impression they made no such distinction at all" (legato and detached), "worried it could be burying a relevant aural feature of polyphonic Bach music"; "try something different with the N64 presets"; "a vocal based preset (or a couple of them) … inspired by this Asian vocal synths"; the organ "real bad too, flat and dull. Why would anyone choose it?!". **Legato and detached** (all sounds). Measured before (I/2's subject alone, Keller's reading, offline through chip.ts; the dip is the lowest level about the second onset against the first note's): no sound told them apart. The others' notes all re-attacked; a "joined" note was let go before the next (its release, 40-120 ms, inside its written length), a detached one held 0.88-1.0: CLASSIC CHIP's joined pairs dipped -30.5 dB and its detached -32.8 (a gap at both), MEGA FM's -3.4 and -6.2, SWITCHED-ON's -4.0 and -5.2 (no gap at either), CHAMBER ORGAN's -32.1 and -36.7; the readings' marks reached only the player's own notes; HEAR THE SUBJECT held a joined note 4% over its length, let it go 120 ms before its end (the player's release) and attacked the next anew; a joined note (Play.legato, the player's) skipped only the level's attack on the synthesized sounds: the filter opened anew, MEGA FM's index burst bright, a recording was attacked anew unless held. Now (phrasing.ts ARTICULATION, `Shape.art/legato/over/from/final`, `joins`; chip.ts `Play.tie/from/final`): each of the others' notes joined (held to the next onset and 30 ms over, the nudges allowed for, crossfaded over its last 15 ms; the next with no fresh attack: the level comes in over 15-20 ms, no filter opening, no FM-index burst, no chiff, no scoop, no consonant; a recording started past its attack; a plucked or struck note plucked anew, the one before held into it: overlegato), detached (held 0.62: the readings' detached, as HEAR THE SUBJECT plays them), short (0.35), nonlegato (0.74; the harpsichord's 0.72 repeated, 0.8 leaps) or plain (before a rest, as before); a gap at most 0.4 of a beat. The readings' marks (Book I) now go on every entry of the subject in every voice (`readingMarks`), as the player is asked to play it; without a reading one rule, after the readings' own manner (steps slurred; leaps, repeated notes and a moving bass apart): a step joined (a third too among notes shorter than a beat), a leap of a fourth or more and a repeated note nonlegato, the lowest voice's notes of a third of a beat to a beat nonlegato (C.P.E. Bach's Versuch, Part I, ch. 3, holds notes neither slurred nor detached about half their value; ours longer, 0.74, so the lines hold on a phone). Never louder: the readings' accents are not levels (D293). Also: a plucked synthesized note damped in 30 ms (80 ms filled a detached note's gap), a held recording's release done by its written end (it rang 0.2 s past it). Measured after (same renders): joined dips -0.2 to -4.1 dB with onset slopes 0.6-7.5 dB per 4 ms (a plucked sound re-plucks: FOREST 64's guitar 13.8), detached dips -11 to -89 dB (TOMITA -11, PIPE ORGAN -21, SUPER 16-BIT -22: their echoes and rooms in the gaps), e.g. CLASSIC CHIP joined -1.5 / detached -37.6 dB with a 126 ms gap, MEGA FM -1.2 / -35.7, SWITCHED-ON -3.2 / -40.1. Tests on the fake audio graph (its automation now evaluated, FakeParam.valueAt): a joined pair with no dip under 0.6 and no new attack, a detached pair silent (-30 dB) for a quarter of the note at least, on SWITCHED-ON, MEGA FM, NOCTURNE 32-BIT's violin and the harpsichord (a fifth there: its damper and key-up noise). **PIPE ORGAN** (CHAMBER ORGAN remade, id kept): the VCSL organ's recordings where they serve (the plenum, Principal 8' to a mixture, on top; the pedal, reeds and principals at 16' and 8', in the bass, retuned up to B3, the plenum above), made over at 22 kHz, 16 bits, the 120 Hz floor (sampled.ts manualOrg, pedalOrg); between them two synthesized registrations: the flutes (Gedackt 8', Rohrflöte 4' +1.8 cents, Nasard 2 2/3' -1.4, Blockflöte 2', a soft Terz: the Sesquialtera's colour, a phone hears its upper ranks; breath, a strong chiff; the alto of four) and a reed (Trompete 8' with a Principal 4' +1.6 cents, its brassy filter blip; the tenor of four; Krummhorn 8' with a Gedackt -1.8 cents the second of five); each voice's pipes a cent off the others' (`drift`); a large church ("cathedral": 45 ms pre-delay, RT60 2.8 s, 1.8 s of impulse on a phone, 4.8 kHz damping), sent 0.14 (pedal) to 0.28 (flutes). D249 withdrew the sampled organ for a phone's CPU: here two voices of four are recorded (8 nodes a note), the synthesized ones 13-21 nodes; the phone's cap for sustained recordings (18 notes) applies; the worst piece's recordings 13 MB. **FOREST 64** and **PARTY 64** in place of TEMPLE 64 and AGENT 64 (RETIRED: a stored choice opens on its successor), not muffled (the old: 22 kHz, lowpassed at 4.5-6 kHz, 10 bits, slow pads, a 2.6 s cave): recordings at 26 kHz, softened at 8.5-9.5 kHz, 12 bits. FOREST 64 (after the console's forest and village music): an ocarina (a breathy sine, tongued onset, late vibrato), an FM marimba (second of five), the classical guitar's pluck, the viola section with vibrato, a bassoon (a harp and a pizzicato bass were tried: 11-17 dB weaker than the top through the modelled phone speaker; the bassoon -3.6 dB); a grove (RT60 1.8 s). PARTY 64 (its party and platform games): a bouncy pulse-and-saw lead (its filter popping open, a 12 ms blip from above), a muted trumpet (five voices), the clean electric guitar, a sax, the picked electric bass; a short bright room (RT60 1.0 s). **VOCAL SYNTH** (after the character singing synthesizers; named for what it is): five singers by formant synthesis (chip.ts singNote, sound.ts `Sing`, VOWELS, CONSONANTS): a nearly flat pulse train and breath through five parallel formant band-passes (the classic singing-formant table, soprano/alto/tenor/bass by vowel), each voice its own type, size, vowel and consonant: "la" (a bright young soprano: +5 dB about 3 kHz, vibrato 32 cents at 6 Hz from 0.3 s), "mi" (second of five), "ro" (a mezzo), "ne" (a tenor), "da" (a bass, no vibrato); a new syllable comes in from its consonant (formants from the consonant's into the vowel's, a plosive's burst, a nasal's murmur, a tap's dip) with the pitch scooped 40-80 cents from below, over 8-18 above and settling; a joined note keeps the vowel and glides from the note before (Play.from); a phrase's last note falls 60-120 cents. About 23 nodes a note (two sources and an LFO). **SOLO VOICES**: VocalSet's soprano, mezzo, tenor and bass singing "a" (CC BY 4.0, credited as the piano is) and FluidR3's choir "aah" (second of five), each its own syllable (la, ma, na, ra, da: the voice opening from a lowpass, a burst for the d), the pitch scooped or glided (the recordings' detune), their own vibrato; tuning measured (median over the 1-3 s loops); 23 MB at worst. Both in a bright plate (RT60 1.5 s). **The low-graded** ("boring, dull, flat"): MEGA FM remade (the modulator fed back on itself, `fm.fb`, operator 1's bright saw-like edge; a few cents off its ratio, `fm.detune`, DT1's slow beating; a second modulator's own fall, `mod2.to/time`: a fed-back lead, brass, an e-piano with a 1:14 tine, a clav, a slap bass with a click and a 30-cent snap; the 9-bit grain kept, the lowpass 7 → 10 kHz, a short dark stereo echo, "echo"); SUPER 16-BIT lusher (violin, flute, harpsichord now in the echo, the viola section, bassoon; 22 kHz, 6-7.5 kHz, sustains falling over 4 s; the SNES echo longer and in stereo: 160/240 ms, crossing over, feedback 0.4, 3.2 kHz; a pizzicato tried for the plucked voice, 7-8 dB weaker on a phone, not kept); TRACKER punchier (a volume slide on each sample, the tone portamento into a joined note, `Patch.slide`; the 8-bit choir "aah" and a slapped bass in place of the clarinet and the bassoon; hard left and right at ±0.35); TOMITA more colourful (the whistle's portamento, phased strings and choir, `Patch.phaser`, filters sweeping with the clock, `filter.sweep`, the flute and the whistle wandering across the stereo, `Patch.autopan`, within ±0.35; the sweeps follow the clock, so go on from note to note, `clocked`). The 6s left as they were (their articulation aside). Measured (offline renders through chip.ts, the harness as D297's; integrated LUFS; phone = touch emulated through the modelled speaker, a 600 Hz high-pass): II/22 54-74 s, all four voices, the seventeen -20.9 (VOCAL SYNTH) to -22.7 (GAME BOY, NOCTURNE 32-BIT); the changed: PIPE ORGAN -21.1, FOREST 64 -21.4, PARTY 64 -21.2, VOCAL SYNTH -20.9, SOLO VOICES -21.6, MEGA FM -21.9, SUPER 16-BIT -21.2, TRACKER -21.9, TOMITA -22.0; on a phone each voice alone, the bass against the soprano: PIPE ORGAN +1.2 dB, FOREST 64 -3.6, PARTY 64 -2.3, VOCAL SYNTH -1.8, SOLO VOICES +1.5, MEGA FM -4.2, SUPER 16-BIT -4.9, TRACKER -0.7, TOMITA -3.8 (every voice of the new five within 5.2 dB of the others); I/2's first 20 s -23.2 to -26.9 (CLASSIC CHIP -31.0, -30.0 before). Phone memory (every piece, all a preset's recordings): PIPE ORGAN 13 MB, FOREST 64 13, PARTY 64 22, SOLO VOICES 23 (the budget 96). Measured, not heard: whether the singers sound like singers and the organ like a church; the vocal and organ costs on a phone (counted in nodes, not timed on one; offline on the machine that rendered them, II/22's 22.5 s took 6.9 s on VOCAL SYNTH, the costliest, 5.6 s on TOMITA, 5.0 s on PIPE ORGAN with its recordings' decoding, 2.1 s on CLASSIC CHIP). | phrasing.ts ARTICULATION, Art, ART_HOLD, GAP_BEATS, OVERLAP_S, Marks, phrase, joins, Shape; readings.ts readingMarks, performSubject (legato, tie, from, final), JOIN_OVER_S; chip.ts Play.tie/from/final, voiceNote, patchNote (legato: no filter/index/scoop, tie, slide, phaser, filter sweep, autopan, fm fb/detune/mod2 fall, damper 30 ms), sampledNote (tie, release by the end, syllable, slide), singNote, SING_GAIN, feedback, ECHOES, SPC_ECHO, FM_ECHO, REVERBS (cathedral, grove, room64, plate), clocked; sound.ts PATCHES org*/f64*/p64*/voc*/sing*/fm*/s16*/trk*/tom*, Sing, VOWELS, CONSONANTS, SHAPES glottal/stopTrompete/stopFlute4, Patch slide/phaser/autopan/sing/syllable/fm, FxKind, PRESETS, RETIRED, creditFor, VOICES_CREDIT, CHOIR_CREDIT; sampled.ts PERIOD, SAMPLED, VSCO_CENTS, SHORT; proto-game.ts, engine.ts, BenchApp.tsx, hear-sound.ts; PlayPop.tsx POP_SOUNDS; i18n; test/d302-articulation.test.ts, d302-presets.test.ts, fake-audio (valueAt, merger), d297-presets, chip-presets, bench-pop-sounds, bt-mode, bench-d191 | WTC 1.27 |

| D304 | The owner rated D302's sounds on an iPhone (CLASSIC CHIP, GAME BOY, SID 64 10; harpsichord, piano 9; NOCTURNE 32-BIT, VOCAL SYNTH, SOLO VOICES 8; SWITCHED-ON, TOMITA 7; PIPE ORGAN, MEGA FM, SUPER 16-BIT, TRACKER 6; MODULAR 2; FOREST 64, PARTY 64 0): "ON THE ARTICULATIONS: I feel like this was a bad design idea.
